CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	financial markets - wholesale sector	the professional sector or institutional sector. It is the business-to-business (B2B) part of the industry where both sides of a transaction are businesses, not individuals.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	activities in the wholesale sector	Equity markets<br>bond markets<br>FX<br>derivatives (options swaps futures forwards)<br>insurance markets<br>fund management (collective investment schemes (CISs)<br>investment banking<br>custodial banking	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	Fund management&nbsp;	managing the investment portfolios of collective investment schemes (CISs), pension funds, endowments or insurers' funds. This includes ‘alternative’ investments generally only available to institutions or other large investors&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	Investment banking	banking services tailored to organisations, such as undertaking mergers and acquisitions, marketing and underwriting new issues of securities for raising capital, equity and fixed income trading, and over-the-counter (OTC) derivatives trading.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	retail sector	sector focuses on individuals and households. It is business-to-consumer (B2C) and includes:	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	Retail banking&nbsp;	the traditional range of current accounts, deposit accounts, lending and credit cards.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	equity markets	facilitate the trading of shares in quoted or listed (public) companies. Equity markets involve both institutional and individual investors, though institutional investors dominate trading activity (such as asset managers) who trade the shares held in their clients’ funds, and insurance companies.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	how does equity trading work	The arranged trade will often involve purchasing from, or selling to, a bank or specialised firm which is acting as a dealer or, in some cases, as a market maker. As larger firms provide their clients with the capacity to both arrange and to buy equities from them, or sell equities to them, such firms are often termed brokers/dealers.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	what is global market capitalisation	US$101.5 trillion as of September 2025 (note that not all stock exchanges provide data to the World Federation of Exchanges so actual figures will be higher).&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY			
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	Global market capitalisation&nbsp;	total value of shares listed on the world’s stock exchanges.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	what is the largest exchange in the world	Nasdaq US&nbsp;<br>market capitalisation of US$33.15 trillion as of end July 2025&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	what is the 2nd largest	New York Stock Exchange (NYSE)<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	3rd largest exchange	Shanghai Stock Exchange (SSE) (US$7.96 trillion),&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	MFTs/ATSs	multilateral trading facilities, alternative trading systems<br>MTFs and ATSs are systems that bring together multiple parties that are interested in buying and selling financial instruments such as shares. These systems are also known as crossing networks or matching engines, and are provided by either an investment firm or another market operator.&nbsp;<br>(think bloomberg RFQ)<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	interdealer brokers	inter-dealer brokers (IDBs) or other intermediaries may operate their own proprietary trade platforms, sometimes called ‘dark pools’ or electronic communications networks (ECNs) which also compete with exchanges for trading volume.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	Bond markets	little dealing is done via stock exchange systems, with the majority of trades taking place away from the exchanges in OTC trades. Furthermore, the trading volume is lower, eg, a smaller number of larger trades as most trades tend to be very large when compared to equity market trades. The amount outstanding on the global bond market at the end of 2024 reached US$145.1 trillion.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	where is the largest bond market	US, however most trading is concentrated in europe	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	FX markets	Forex markets are open 24 hours a day, 7 days a week. Trading currency pairs<br><br>rate at which one currency is exchanged for another is driven by supply and demand. For example, if there is strong demand from Japanese investors for US assets, such as property, bonds or shares, the US dollar will tend to rise in value relative to the Japanese yen.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	what kind of market is FX&nbsp;	FX is an OTC market, meaning one where brokers/dealers negotiate directly with one another, there is no central exchange or clearing house. Instead, FX trading is distributed among major financial centres. One minor exception is that there are certain FX futures and options contracts which are traded on a few of the exchanges.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	where is most active for FX trading	London is the most active financial centre in the world for FX trading.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	retail banks services	taking deposits from, and lending funds to, retail customers, as well as providing payment and money transmission services.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	pension funds	Pension funds are large, long-term investors in assets such as shares, bonds and cash. Some also invest in physical assets such as property or alternative assets such as venture capital or hedge funds.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	insurance companies	effectively manage risks. Insurance companies provide life products, which pay a named beneficiary when the insured dies, and general insurance, which covers risks arising from incidents, such as accidents and theft&nbsp;<br>Insurance companies collect premiums in exchange for the cover provided. This premium income is used to buy investments, such as shares and bonds, and as a result, the insurance industry is a major player in the stock market. Insurance companies will subsequently realise these investments to pay any claims that may arise on the various policies.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	investment services	specialists have emerged to provide services around the investments for customers, generally for a fee.<br>growing source of retail investment advice comes from ‘robo-advisors’. Robo-advisors use algorithms to provide automated investment advice at a relatively low fee. A customer fills out an online assessment and the robo-advisor makes recommendations based on the customer’s answers, and re-balances the portfolio to stay on track as necessary. Robo-advisors are often subsidiaries of traditional financial institutions or of large technology firms.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	Financial Planning and Wealth Management	Wealth managers do more than just provide investment advice. They consider the client’s total financial situation and charge a fee for their advice and commission on transactions. They may also look after client assets and charge custody and portfolio management fees.<br>financial planning is a professional service available to individuals, their families and businesses who need objective assistance in organising their financial affairs to achieve their financial and lifestyle objectives more easily.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	investment banks	provide advice and arrange finance for companies that want to float or list on a stock market, raise additional finance by issuing shares or bonds, or assist companies in carrying out mergers and acquisitions. They also provide services for those who might want to invest in shares and bonds, for example, pension funds and asset managers.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	services provided by IBs	Corporate finance and advisory work,&nbsp;<br>Banking, for governments, institutions and companies.<br>Treasury dealing for corporate clients in foreign currencies, with financial engineering services to protect them from interest rates and help them manage their exchange rate risk.&nbsp;<br>Investment management for sizeable investors, such as corporate pension funds, charities and private clients.&nbsp;<br>Securities trading in equities, bonds and derivatives, and the provision of broking and distribution facilities.<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	fund managers	professional management of investment portfolios for a variety of institutions and private investors.<br><br>The US is the largest centre globally for fund management, followed by the UK, which is the biggest in Europe.<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	what do fund managers do	run portfolios of investments for others. They invest money held by institutions, such as pension funds and insurance companies, as well as for collective investment schemes (CISs),&nbsp;<br><br>Investment managers who buy and sell shares, bonds and other assets in order to increase the value of their clients’ portfolios&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	institutional fund managers	Institutional fund managers work on behalf of institutions in the wholesale/professional sector, for example, investing money for a company’s pension fund or an insurance company’s fund.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	private client fund managers	Private client fund managers invest the money of relatively wealthy individuals in the retail sector. Institutional portfolios are usually larger than those of regular private clients.&nbsp;<br><br>Fund managers charge their clients for managing their money; these charges are often based on a small percentage of the value of the fund being managed. They may also charge an incentive fee, based on investment performance above a benchmark.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	hedge funds	alternative fund managers, which may invest in non-traditional assets such as private equity, or which trade complex strategies and take both long and short positions (eg, ‘hedge funds’).	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	brokers	brokers arrange trades in financial instruments on behalf of their clients, which include institutional brokers in the wholesale market that earn revenue by executing large trades with discretion and skill.<br>broking activities are undertaken by divisions of large investment banks rather than independent entities and, like retail brokers, they earn revenue by charging commissions and/or profiting from the bid-ask spread on trades executed.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	custodian banks	banks that specialise in safe custody services, looking after shares, bonds and cash on behalf of others (eg, fund managers, pension funds and insurance companies) often in multiple currencies.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	what is asset allocation	"<span style=""color: rgb(142, 102, 46); font-family: -apple-system, BlinkMacSystemFont, &quot;Segoe UI&quot;, Roboto, &quot;Helvetica Neue&quot;, Arial, &quot;Noto Sans&quot;, &quot;Liberation Sans&quot;, sans-serif, &quot;Apple Color Emoji&quot;, &quot;Segoe UI Emoji&quot;, &quot;Segoe UI Symbol&quot;, &quot;Noto Color Emoji&quot;; font-size: 15px; background-color: rgb(252, 239, 220);"">The distribution of investments across different asset classes</span>"	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 1: THE FINANICAL SERVICES INDUSTRY	bearer securities	not registered securities, hard to track who they belong to	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	equity	shares of ownsership in companies	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	fixed income	investment with fixed return like bonds - lower risk	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	FICC	fixed income, currencies, commodities	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	alt investments	less liquid for diversification - eg real estate crypto dividends	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	types of shares	ordinary and preference shares	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	ordinary shares	common stock, equity in company with voting rights, entitled to dividends	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	types of ordinary shares	different types with different voting powers, can also be non - voting rights common stock, may trade at a discount	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	redeemable shares	shares which can be bought back by a company at their discretion	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	preference shares	less risky shares, usually no voting rights and entitled to a fixed dividend each year - like a fixed income product but equity. they rank above ordinary shares in the event of bankrupcy	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	dividend rules - shares	preference shares must be paid divs before ordinary shares, and its a fixed amount.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	priority tower in bankrupcy	bond holders paid first, then preference shares, then ordinary shares	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	risk of liquidation to ordinary shareholders	If the company is liquidated, they will only receive a pay-out if there is money remaining after satisfying all of the other claims from creditors, bondholders and preference shareholders.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	a proxy	individual or group of individuals appointed by the board of directors of the company to represent the shareholders who send in proxy requests, to vote the represented shares in accordance with the shareholders’ instructions.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	cumulative preference shares	receive dividends before ordinary shares and the dividends from the time period before	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	participating preference shares	preference shares with voting rights	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	participating preference shares	entitled to a raised dividend in companies good years. normal preference shares have a fixed dividend, participating have the oppurtunity to make more if company does well.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	redeemable preference shares	preference shares that enable the company to buy back the shares from the shareholder at an agreed price in the future.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	convertible preference shares	convertible shareholder has the right to change out preference shares for ordinary shares if they wish.&nbsp;method of avoiding the lack of upside potential in the preference shares, compared to ordinary shares.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	zero coupon preference shares	preference shares that pay no dividend, but offer an upside to the shareholder in that they redeem at a price above that at which they are issued.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	types of preference shares	<div>The four major types of preference share are:</div><div>•&nbsp; &nbsp; Cumulative</div><div>•&nbsp; &nbsp; Participating</div><div>•&nbsp; &nbsp; Redeemable</div><div>•&nbsp; &nbsp; Convertible</div><div>A further form of preference share is the zero- dividend preference share that pays no dividend.</div><div><br></div>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	features of a bond	will have a face value, a coupon, and an amount of time till matrity when it will be repaid.<br><br>face value is known as par, or nominal value -<br>&nbsp;coupon is the yield, amount that paid to you each year of the value.&nbsp;<br>time to maturity is when the bond is repaid	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	issuer	whoever is borrowing the money/selling the bond	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	what determines the coupon of a bond	more risky=higher coupon, higher yield as higher probability of deafult	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	registered securities	registered certificates require that the holder’s ownership is recorded in a digital register as the owner (or title-holder) of the investment.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	bearer securities	the person that bears (or holds) them has title to them,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	where are bearer securities held	held in central securities depositories (CSDs), such as Euroclear and Clearstream, and are technically referred to as being ‘immobilised’.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	flat yield	flat yield only considers the coupon and ignores the existence of any capital gain (or loss) if the bond is held through to redemption<br><br>doesnt consider rise in value of bond just yield.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	flat yield equation	Flat yield = (annual coupon/price) x 100	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	why is flat yield useful	simple to see how a change in interest rates will impact bond prices. If interest rates increase, investors will want an equivalent increase in the yield on their bonds. However, because the coupon is fixed for most bonds, the only way that the yield can increase is for the price to fall.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	Gross Redemption Yield (GRY)/Yield to Maturity (YTM)&nbsp;	fuller measure of yield than the flat yield because it takes both the coupons and any gain (or loss) through to maturity into account&nbsp;<br>i.e includes execution fees	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	net redemption yield	takes both the annual coupons and the profit (or loss) made through to maturity into account. However, it looks at the after-tax cash flows good for tax paying investors	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	modified duration	shows the expected change in its price, given a specified change in interest rates. The higher the modified duration, the more the price of that instrument will move. The modified duration is the approximate percentage change in the price of a bond brought about by a 1% change in the interest rate.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	examples of modified duration	If a government bond is priced at $95.84, and its modified duration is 1.02, what is the effect on the price after an increase in interest rates by one percentage point?<div><br></div><div>If interest rates rise by one percentage point, the bond’s price will fall by 1.02 / 100 x $95.84 = $0.98.</div><div><br></div><div>If interest rates rise by one half of a percentage point, the bond’s price will fall by 1.02 / 100 x $95.84 x 0.5 = $0.49.</div>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	convertible bonds	bonds give the holder of the bond the right, but not the obligation, to convert the bond into a predetermined number of ordinary shares of the issuer.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	how do convertible bonds trade	at a premium - your downside is minimised by the nominal value of the yield, but if the companies share price goes up - you can exchange to ordinary shares - built in option instrument.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	rule of 72	how long something takes to double - divide 72 by the return your getting	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	how do fixed income assets move	if interest rates rise - price of debt falls (demand goes as interest beats the yield) yields will rise<br><br>if interest rates fall - price of debt rises (demand for FI goes up and price rises) yields will fall	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	internal rate of return&nbsp;	The IRR is simply the discount rate that, when applied to the future cash flows of the bond, produces the current price of that bond.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	why do bonds trade at a premium	if the coupon is higher that the interest rate, the bond will trade at a premium&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	conversion ratio	nominal value/conversion price of shares	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	conversion premium	difference in redeemable share amount and trading value of bond/conversion value	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	flat yield equation	(annual coupon rate/market price) X 100	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	limits of flat yields	often gives an incomplete picture of the actual returns from the bond. A bond that has been purchased at a price that is below the redemption value will be significantly undervalued because the redemption gain is excluded from the calculation. The opposite is also true when a bond is purchased at a price above the redemption value.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	bond clean price	the flat price of the bond, listed price does not include interest	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	Accrued interest&nbsp;	interest that has been earned, but not paid,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	accrued interest equation	Accrued interest = Coupon payment x (Number of days since last payment / Number of days between payments)	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	dirty price	price of bond inclusive for accrued interest	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	dirty price	market price including accrued interest	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	spread in bond markets	difference between two yields, usually expressed in basis points, with each basis point representing 1/100 of 1%.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	what are spreads used for	used to compare instruments to one a other and benchmarks	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	what are corporate bonds benchmarked to	gov bonds with similar issuance year and YTM	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	why would spreads vary	<div><br></div><div>Spreads will vary, mainly as a result of the relative risk of the corporate bond issuer compared to the government, or the financial institutions providing the swaps. For a riskier corporate issuer, the spread will be greater.</div>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	yield curve	maps yields on Y axis to time on X axis - follows logarithmic shape (flattens at top), yields higher for longer term rates	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	inverted yield curve	yields available on short-term government bonds exceed those available on long-term government bonds.&nbsp;<br>occurs when there is an expectation of a significant reduction in interest rates at some stage in the future.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	negative interest rate	in financial crisis - so banks have to pay to leave their cash with the central bank - forces investment	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	real yields	adjusted for inflation - products such as TIPS and index linked GILTS against nominal yields show inflation outlook	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	present value	value of the bond adjusted for time value -&nbsp;1 / (1 + r)n with r being the IR	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	example of present value calculation	$100 receivable in one year’s time, given an interest rate of 5%.<br><br>$100 x 1 / (1+r)n = $100 x 1 / (1+0.05)1 = $100 x 1 / 1.05 = $100 x 0.9524 = $95.24<br><br>$100 due in a year, so 95.24 is price now.<br><br>in 2 years:<br><br>$100 x 1 / (1+r)2 = $100 x 1 / (1.05)2 = $100 x 1 / 1.1025 = $100 x 0.907 = $90.70	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	how to calculate a bond price	calculate the yearly present value cash flows, add together these amounts up to maturity - this gives the present value of the total bond&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	what is IR and coupon are the same	bond trades at Par value - as present value is equal&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	how are government bonds prices quoted	quoted on the basis of the price a buyer would pay for 100 units of the currency’s nominal value.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	index linked bonds	Index-linked bonds, such as UK index-linked gilts (ILGs) and US Treasury inflation protected securities (TIPS), differ from conventional bonds in that the coupon payments and the principal are adjusted in line with a published index of price inflation, such as the retail prices index (RPI) or the consumer prices index (CPI).&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	what is the effect on inflation	inflation reduces the spending power of those dependent on fixed incomes, such as pensions or fixed-coupon investments, including conventional bonds<br>inflation increases the cost of living today, which competes with cashflow that could be used to invest, making it more difficult for individuals to invest the amount needed to meet investment goals<br>individuals may not be rewarded for saving; this occurs when the inflation rate exceeds the nominal interest rate, ie, when the real interest rate is negative.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	consumer price index	calculated each month by looking at the goods and services that a typical household might buy, including food, heating, household goods and travel costs, and comparing the increase in their costs to a base year.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	retail price index	average measure of change in the prices of goods and services. Once published, it is never revised.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	producer prices index	inflation at an earlier stage in the production process. PPIs include input price indices that measure the change in prices going into the production process, including raw materials and other inputs.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	SRTIPS	separate trading of registered interest and principle of securities	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	zero coupon bonds	promise to pay just the nominal value at redemption. As there is no other possible form of return, investors will pay less than the nominal value (they will buy them at a discount) when they buy ZCBs, with their return coming in the form of the difference between the discounted price they pay for the bond and the amount they receive when the bond is redeemed.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	stripping a bond	Stripping a bond involves trading the interest (each individual coupon) and the principal (the nominal value) separately.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	where have STRIPS markets grown	STRIPS markets have been developed in US Treasuries and in the UK gilts market and in Japan for JGBs.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	advantage of STRIPS	investors can precisely match their liabilities, removing any reinvestment risk.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	BRIC countries	branch of emerging markets - brazil russia india china, recently become BRICS with south afirca	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	risk of investing in EM/Frontier bonds	variables of the issuer’s economic or financial performance and the ability of the issuer to meet payment obligations. These risks are heightened due to the potential political and economic volatility of these developing nations - higher credit risk	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	corporate debt	money that is borrowed by a company that has to be repaid. Generally, corporate debt also requires servicing by making regular interest payments.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	asset backed security	collateral to a bond/loan	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	types of ABS types	Fixed charge – the debt carries a charge over a particular company asset, eg, a building, and<div><br></div><div>Floating charge – the debt is secured against a group of the company’s assets; in the event of default, a floating charge crystallises over the available assets at that point.</div>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	ABSs	Asset-backed securities (ABSs) are bonds that are backed by a particular pool of assets. These assets can take several forms, such as mortgage loans, credit card receivables and car loans.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	securitisation	The assets provide the bondholders’ collateral, since the cash generated from them is used to service the bonds (pay the interest), and to repay the principal sum at maturity. Such arrangements are often referred to as the securitisation of assets.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES			
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	MBSs	type of ABS containing mortgages -&nbsp;created from mortgage loans made by financial institutions&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	special purpose vehicle	remote vehicle off a companies balance sheet for making ABSs - to remove default risk	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	what form does an SPV usually take	SPV is often a trust, and the originator of the assets, such as the bank granting the mortgage loans, sells the loans to the SPV and the SPV issues the asset-backed bonds.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	purposes of the SPV	SPV is a separate entity from the originator of the assets, so the assets leave the originator’s financial statements to be replaced by the cash from the SPV.&nbsp;<br><br>SPV is a stand-alone entity, so, if the originator of the assets suffers bankruptcy, the SPV still remains intact with the pool of assets available to service the bonds.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	what happens when no SPV is used	where no SPV is created, the asset-backed bonds are simply referred to as ‘covered bonds’, referring to the pool of assets that provide the ‘cover’ to the bondholder.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	debenture	in the uk, means a secured debt transaction. in the us, loan agreement with no security at all. - ABS is the term in the US for secured debt transactions	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	trustee - securitised loans	when a us/uk company issues a lot of secured debt, a trustee is appointed	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	why is a trustee appointed for secured debt&nbsp;	trust deed, the property of the company is mortgaged to the holders of the securities to secure the payment of the money owed.&nbsp;<br><br>there is a deed of trust in place between the company and trustees -&nbsp;The trustee holds the benefit of the covenant by the company to repay the monies on trust for the holders of the securities.<br><br>trustees make sure the companies pay the securitised debt holders	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	trustee roles for a UK debenture trustee&nbsp;	note trustee<br>security trustee<br>share trustee<br>successor trustee<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	note trustee	represents interests of the security holders, advices the issuer<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	security trustee	for issues secured by a pledge of securities or other properties,&nbsp;<br>charged in favour of the trustee for the benefit of the various secured parties.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	share trustee	holds the shares in an issuing SPV in order to ensure off-balance-sheet treatment for the originator of the transaction.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	successor trustee	provided for banks which need to resign because of conflicts of interest (especially in connection with defaulted or bankrupt issues) or when work requirements exceed the bank’s capacity.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	benefits of a trust deed	enables a coherent enforcement procedure, rather than a series of disparate actions by different holders of securities.&nbsp;<br>ensures organised action and parity of treatment.&nbsp;<br>makes sure all holders are paid fairly	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	bondholders trustee	trustee is assigned to represent the interests of the bondholders. The corporate trustee is usually a bank or a trust company, and, although the trustee is paid by the issuing corporation, it represents the interests of the bondholders.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	why are rating agencies used	to assess the creditworthiness of the issuer and the likelihood that all of the terms of the securities offering are likely to be fulfilled.&nbsp;<br>engagement is to represent the interest of the bondholders,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	problem with rating agencies	paid by the issuer - so can be skewed	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	corporate trustee role	must keep track of all bonds sold, verifying that the amount issued is not greater than what is stated in the indenture and making sure that the corporation complies with all covenants&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	tiers of debt	represent who gets paid first in insolvency - 3 teirs.<br><br>senior debt<br>sub ordinated<br>mezzanine	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	senior debt	debt ranks above all other debt and equity capital in the business. paid first<br>Senior debt often has to meet strict covenants, including meeting or exceeding certain minimum financial ratios, and it may be secured against specific assets of the company. It will have the lowest interest rate of all the tiers&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	subordinated debt	debt ranks behind senior debt&nbsp;<br>requirements of the subordinated debt are generally less stringent&nbsp;<br>higher interest rate	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	mezzanine debt	high-risk subordinated debt, lowest rank<br>interest on mezzanine debt tends to be much higher than subordinated debt.&nbsp;<br>interest might not be paid in cash and is added to the principal outstanding instead.&nbsp;<br>mezzanine debt will also include warrants or options so that the lender can participate in equity returns.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	payment in kind interest	interest might not be paid in cash and is added to the principal outstanding instead. This rolled up interest is often described as ‘payment-in-kind (PIK)’ interest,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	unsecured debt	Unsecured debt is not secured against any of the company’s assets, so the holder has no special protection in the event of default.&nbsp;<br>this is compensated by a higher coupon rate than secured debt	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	what types of debt is usecured	Subordinated debt is typically unsecured and the lenders have agreed that, if the company fails, they will only be repaid once other creditors have been repaid, if there is enough money left over.&nbsp;<br><br>Unsecured debtholders, however, would still be repaid prior to shareholders, as ‘lenders’ are always repaid before ‘owners’ in an insolvency.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	guaranteed debt	guarantee is provided by someone other than the issuer. The guarantor is typically the parent company, or another company in the same group of companies as the issuer.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	convertible bonds	give the holder of the bond the right, but not the obligation, to convert into a predetermined number of ordinary shares of the issuer.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	3 types of unsecured debt	convertible<br>guaranteed<br>subordinated	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	fixed coupon bonds	bonds are issued with a fixed rate of coupon. If interest rates rise, the fixed coupon becomes less attractive and the price of the bond falls.&nbsp;<br><br>for government bonds, the interest is always calculated by reference to the nominal value of the bond, so a $1,000 nominal 5% ABC corporate bond will pay $50 pa to the holder.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	floating rate notes	Floating-rate bonds or notes are bonds when the coupon rate varies. The rate is adjusted in line with published, market interest rates.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	what floating rates are used for floating rate notes	SONIA in UK, SOFR in the US, and EURIBOR in the EU.<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	FRNs magrins	FRNs typically add a margin to the reference rate (such as SOFR or SONIA),&nbsp;<br><br>e.g.&nbsp;corporate issuer may offer floating-rate bonds to investors at SONIA plus 175 basis points. If SONIA is at 2%, the coupon paid will be 3.75%,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	credit/default risk	risk that bondholders face&nbsp;issuer of the bond might default on their obligation to pay interest and the principal amount at redemption.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	rating agencies	Standard and Poor’s (S&amp;P), Moody’s and Fitch Ratings inc.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	types of grades	investment grade rating and those categorised as non-investment grade or speculative.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	high-yield or junk bonds.&nbsp;	non-investment grade or speculative rated bonds	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	features of investment grade bonds	offer the greatest liquidity and security, meaning the issuer is likely to meet both interest and principal repayments. In exchange for this assurance, however, they typically pay lower interest rates.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	features of junk bonds	Bonds with lower credit ratings tend to pay higher coupons to compensate for the higher risk that investors take by investing in them. Further, if ratings change, the price of bonds which have already been issued will be adjusted to reflect the fact that they have become more, or less, attractive to investors.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	how do rating agencies determine ratings	by assessing whether the cash flow likely to be generated by the borrower will comfortably service, and ultimately repay, its debts,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	japan credit rating agency&nbsp;	agency that specialises in that specific reigon - japan	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	global credit ratings	GCR - specialises in africa ratings	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	credit enhancement	an&nbsp;insurance scheme that will pay out should the pool of assets be insufficient to service or repay the debt. - used to up the ratings	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	ratings used by moodys	moodys uses numbers and mix of upper and lower case letters, such as Baa1 - lower medium grade<br><br>and Aaa being prime grade<br><br>C in default<br>Caa3 etc	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	ratings used by s&amp;p	uses all capitals, and +/- signs<br><br>such as AAA,AA+&nbsp;<br>short term uses A=1+,A-1,A-1,A-3,B,C	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	ratings used by Fitch	"uses basically the same as S&amp;P,&nbsp; but uses ""D"" for in default and F1,F2,F3 for short term debt ratings"	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	Cash assets	market involving cash deposits and short-term instruments that are issued with less than one year to maturity, aka money markets	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	examples of money market instruments	money market instruments are T-bills issued by governments and commercial paper (CP) issued by companies which will generally pay higher interest rates given their longer maturity.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	what compromises cash deposits	Cash deposits comprise accounts held with banks or other savings institutions. They are held by a wide variety of depositors – from retail investors through to companies, governments and financial institutions.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	characteristics of cash depoists	the return simply comprises interest income with no potential for capital growth,<div><br></div><div>the amount invested (the capital) is repaid in full at the end of the investment term.</div>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	advantages to invest in cash	Liquidity – the ease and speed with which an investment can be turned into cash to meet spending needs.&nbsp;<div>Most investors are likely to have a need for cash at short notice and so should plan to hold some on deposit to meet possible needs and emergencies before considering other less liquid investments.</div><div><br></div><div>Interest – cash pays a predictable return in the form of interest.</div><div>Safety – cash is not exposed to market volatility, as is the case with other types of assets.</div>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	problems with investing in cash	Banks and savings institutions are of varying creditworthiness and the risk that they may default needs to be assessed and taken into account.<div>Inflation reduces the real return that is being earned on cash deposits and often the after-tax return can be negative.</div><div>Interest rates vary, and so the returns from cash-based deposits will also vary. In general, cash will provide the lowest returns compared to any other instruments.</div>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	government-sponsored compensation scheme.&nbsp;	protection of cash deposits, in the UK its the FSCS - deposits protected up to £85000	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	T - Bills	treasury bills, short term loans to manage government liquidity<br>short-term loan instruments, issued and guaranteed by the government, with maturity dates ranging between one and 12 months.&nbsp;<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	how often are treasury bills issued	US issues T-bills weekly, with maturities of four weeks, 13 weeks, 26 weeks and 52 weeks.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	feautures of a T-Bill	T-bills pay no coupon and, consequently, are issued at a discount to their nominal value,&nbsp;<br><br>the difference between the par value (maturity value) and the discount value (purchase price) representing the return to the investor.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	benefits of a T-bill	guaranteed by the government, T-bills provide a very secure investment for market participants with short-term investment horizons, and this type of instrument often represents a risk- free investment. The return on a T-bill is wholly dependent upon the price paid.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	commercial paper	short-term promissory note issued primarily by corporations, T bill for companies<br><br>Commercial paper represents a large segment of the money market.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	features of a commercial paper	Commercial paper is issued at a discount to its nominal value and can have a maturity of up to one year in Europe and 270 days in the US; however, it is common to find in both territories that CP will be issued for three months.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	why are CPs used	Large companies issue CP to assist in the management of their liquidity. Rather than borrowing directly from banks,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	asset backed CPs	short-term investment vehicle with a maturity that is typically between 90 and 180 days. The security itself will be issued by a bank or other financial institution, and the notes are backed by assets, such as receivables.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	methods of issuing CPs	The issuer can market the securities directly to a buy-and-hold investor as with most money market funds.&nbsp;<br><br>it can sell the paper to a dealer, who then sells the paper in the market. The dealer market for CP involves large investment banks and other financial services firms, such as bond dealers.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	dealer paper	a CP programme advertising lots of CPs at different maturity levels	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	direct paper	CPs issued by banks to to ability to market directly to investors	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	risk of CPs	CP entails credit risk, and programmes are rated by the major rating agencies. Because CP is a rolling form of debt, with new issues generally funding the retirement of old issues, the main risk is that the issuer will not be able to issue new CP. refinancing/rollover risk	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	what is a repo	a sale and repurchase agreement.&nbsp;<br><br><div>It involves an initial sale of a financial instrument and, at the same time, an agreement that the seller will subsequently buy back the same financial instrument for a specified price at a set future date.&nbsp;</div>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	features of a repo contract	a repo is legally binding for both buyer and seller.&nbsp;<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	example of a repo agreement	a government bond repo is a contract in which the seller of government bonds agrees to buy them back at a future specified time and price. In effect, a government bond repo is a means of borrowing using the bond as security,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	repo rate	the difference between the cash a participant pays on the repo start date for the security, and the mount they get back at the repo end date. expressed as a percentage	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	benefits of repo&nbsp;	the bondholder:	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	benefits of a repo transaction to the security holder	they are able to raise finance against the security of the bonds that they hold – potentially a relatively cheap source of short-term finance.&nbsp;<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	benefits of the receiver of securities in a repo transaction	security gained by holding the bonds.&nbsp;<br>may be a market-making firm that has sold government bonds that it does not hold. The repo transaction enables the market-making firm to access the bonds that it requires to meet its settlement obligations. helps running of secondary bond markets	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	reverse repo	used for the transactions taken on the opposite side of the repo.&nbsp;<br><br>short-term secured loan.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	eurobonds	bonds that are issued and sold outside of their home country (the currency of issue does not need to be the euro). They can be issued in any currency as long as it is different to the currency of the place from which they are issued.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	example of a eurobond	a eurobond issued in the UK would be in a currency other than pound sterling, such as the euro or the US dollar, and a eurobond issued in Germany would be in a currency other than the euro, such as the US dollar or the Japanese yen.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	why are eurobonds used	provide a way for organisations to issue debt without being restricted to their own domestic market, and provide investors access and an opportunity to invest in markets and currencies outside their home countries.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	how is a eurobond issued	issuer of a eurobond will appoint, and work with, an investment bank that will act as lead manager to the issue. The issuer and lead manager will together establish details of the proposed issue, crucially including the maturity and coupon. The lead manager will, for a fee, underwrite and guarantee that the bonds will raise a certain amount.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	why is a syndicate used - Eurobonds	for a sizeable eurobond issue, the lead manager will establish a syndicate of banks, each agreeing to sell the eurobonds to their client base and accepting responsibility for a proportion of both the issue and the underwriting.<br><br>– the eurobonds are sold to the clients of the syndicate of banks and the issuer.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	how are Eurobonds issued	issued in bearer form and, because they are issued internationally, they are largely free of national regulation.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	features of Eurobonds different to normal bonds	The absence of national regulation means that eurobonds can pay interest gross, making the buyer responsible for paying their own tax and avoiding withholding tax (WHT)&nbsp;<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	types of eurobonds	There are plain vanilla, fixed-coupon bonds that normally pay the coupons once a year. Additionally, there are ZCBs and other forms of eurobond, such as floating-rate bonds and bonds with coupons that increase over time (stepped bonds).	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	depositaries that hold eurobonds	As bearer documents, it is important that eurobonds are kept safe, and this is often achieved by holding the bonds in depositaries, particularly those maintained by Euroclear and Clearstream.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	what does immobilised mean - Eurobonds	means that the bonds are safely held within a reputable depositary and a buyer is likely to retain the bonds in their immobilised form.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	how are Eurobond trades matched	TRAX system	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	what is the trading mechanism for eurobonds	OTC, with T+2	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	when is T+1 scheduled for europe	For the UK, EU and Switzerland the implementation for the move to T+1 is scheduled for October 2027.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	depositary receipts forms	American depositary receipts (ADRs) and global depositary receipts (GDRs).	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	what are ADRs used for	substantial non-US companies may want to attract US investors to raise funds. ADRs facilitate this process; indeed, they were created to make it easier for Americans to invest in overseas companies.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	what are GDRs used for	depositary receipts that are identical to ADRs, except that they are marketed to appeal to a broader base of investors, some of whom may be based outside the US.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	ADRs	American depositary receipt	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	GDRs	global depositary receipts	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	how are DRs created	created (or sponsored) by the foreign corporation. They will liaise with an investment bank regarding the precise structure of the DR, such as the number or fraction of shares represented by each DR.<br><br>&nbsp;A depository bank will then accept a certain number of underlying shares from the issuer, create the DRs to represent the shares and make these DRs available to US and/or other investors, probably via local brokers.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	grey market trading	purchase and sale of an instrument before its formal release to market	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	example of a grey market	depositary bank selling an American depositary receipt in the 3 month period before its created	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	ADR in simple terms	like an ETF for foreign stocks for Americans, the depositary bank is like the issuer, the bank takes care of all FX stuff tho so u only see dollars	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	stamp duty	0.5% of the price paid to purchase shares. - uk tax on DRs	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	warrant	a warrant is an instrument issued by a company that allows the holder to subscribe for shares in that company at a fixed price over a fixed period. A typical warrant may have a life of several years.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	what does a warrant do for the parties involved	sale of warrants for cash will raise money for the company, and, if the warrants are exercised, then further capital will be raised by the company. Holding the warrant does not entitle the investor to receive dividends or to vote at company meetings, so the capital raised until the warrant is exercised could be considered as free.<br><br>Warrants offer a highly geared investment opportunity for the investor, and they are often issued alongside other investments, rather than sold in their own right.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	covered warrant	warrants issued by firms (usually investment banks), rather than the company whose shares the warrant enables the investor to buy. They are offered in the form of call warrants (giving the investor the right to buy), or put warrants&nbsp;<br><br>like options	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	conversion premium - warrants	the price of the warrant plus the exercise price required to buy the underlying share, less the prevailing share price.<br><br>e.g. warrant (contract price is 4p<br>exercise price is 100p<br>share price is 77p<br><br>conversion premium is 100++4-77=27p	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	property as an investment	illiquid asset as not readily tradeable, valuations differ on location, size, condition<br><br>The supply of land is finite and its availability can be further restricted by legislation and local planning regulations. Therefore, price is predominantly determined by changes in demand.<br><br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	residential property	Residential property is where people live,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	commercial property	used for business purposes; commercial property includes shops and offices, industrial premises and farmland.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	investment in residential property	Range of investment opportunities, including second homes, holiday homes and buy-to-let property	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	investment in commercial property	Size of investment required means direct investment in commercial property is limited to property companies and institutional investors	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	tenancies in residential property	Typically short renewable leases	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	commercial property tenancies	Long-term contracts with periods commonly in excess of five years	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	repairs responsibilities in residential property	Landlord is responsible	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	repairs responsibilities in commerical property	Tenant is usually responsible	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	returns on residential property	Largely linked to capital – increase in house prices	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	retruns on commercial property	Significant component is income return from rental income	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	benefits to property exposure	provided positive real long-term returns allied to low volatility and a reliable stream of income. An exposure to property can provide diversification benefits owing to its low correlation with both traditional and alternative asset classes.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	ways of property exposure	property market through buying-to-let or simply by purchasing a second home.&nbsp;<br><br>or in a portfolio,&nbsp;via a mutual fund or shares in publicly- quoted property companies. Either method adds real estate exposure to a portfolio, which is often seen as a hedge to other asset classes.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	problems with property	property can be subject to prolonged downturns and its lack of liquidity, significant maintenance costs, high transaction costs on transfer and the risk of having commercial property with no tenant (and, therefore, no rental income) really only makes commercial property suitable as an investment for long-term investing institutions, such as pension funds.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	real estate investment trusts	investment trust that specialises in commercial property	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	features of REITs	they provide access to property returns without the disadvantage of double taxation.&nbsp;<br><br>when an investor held property company shares, not only would the company pay corporation tax, but the investor would be liable to tax on dividends and any capital growth. Under the rules for REITs, no corporation tax is payable, provided that certain conditions are met and distributions are instead taxable on the investor.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	tax exempt business conditions - REITS	The property rental business carried on by a UK REIT must contain at least three single rental properties. These can be commercial or residential, and a property includes each separately rented unit in a multi-let property, but must not involve a property representing more than 40% of the total value of the property rental business.<br>the REIT must distribute at least 90% of its property rental business profits by way of dividend.<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	benefits of REITS to investors	give investors access to professional property investment and may provide them with new opportunities, such as the ability to invest in commercial property. This allows them to diversify the risk of holding direct property investments.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	advantages of REITS	remove a further risk from holding direct property, namely liquidity risk, or the risk that the investment will not be able to be readily realised.&nbsp;<br><br>closed-ended funds quoted on stock exchanges, and shares in REITs are bought and sold in the same way as other listed company shares.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	closed ended funds	companies with a fixed size depending on share capital, investment trusts example	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	open ended funds	type of investment such as OEICs or unit trusts, which can expand without limit	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	open ended funds&nbsp;	collective funds pooling the money of investors to buy portfolios of investments, such as stocks or bonds. In return for their money, the investors are given units in the fund. The funds are open-ended because they can grow by selling more units or shrink by buying back and cancelling units. This gives the fund the attraction of liquidity.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	redemption moratoria	This means that investors cannot sell back their units for a period, or an extended redemption period, meaning that they have to be more patient than usual and may need to wait months before receiving the proceeds of the sale of units.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	foreign exchange	collective way of describing all the transactions in which one currency is exchanged for another,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	how is FX traded	purely over-the-counter (OTC) and dominated by banks.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	two types of FX	Spot transactions,Forward transactions	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	Spot transactions	immediate currency deals that are settled within two working days.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	Forward transactions	involve currency deals that are agreed for a future date at a rate of exchange fixed now.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	users of FX market	those using the FX market for the purpose of international trade and those participants wanting to speculate on foreign exchange movements.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	(USD/JPY)	US dollar and Japanese yen&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	(EUR/USD)	Euro and US dollar&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	(USD/CHF),&nbsp;	US dollar and Swiss franc&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	(GBP/USD).	British pound and US dollar&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	typical sterling/dollar spot quote&nbsp;	GBP/USD spot rate 1.1555–1.1645<br><br>Buyer’s rate: £1 buys $1.1555.Seller’s rate: $1.1645 buys £1.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	difference between the buyer’s and seller’s rates&nbsp;	the bid-offer spread. It enables the bank offering the deals to make a profit.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	GBP/USD quote forward	<div>spot&nbsp; $1.1555–$1.1645</div><div>three-month forward 1.00–0.97c pm<br><br></div>‘pm’ stands for premium. It is used when the dollar is going to be more expensive relative to sterling in the future.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	forward rate premium	It is deducted from the quoted spot rate in order to arrive at the forward rate.&nbsp;<br><br>premium is quoted in cents, unlike the spot rate, which is quoted in dollars. So, 1.00 pm is a premium of 1 cent or 0.01 dollars. And 0.97 pm is a premium of 0.97 cents or 0.0097 dollars.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	example of foward rate premium	<div>spot&nbsp; $1.1555–$1.1645</div><div>three-month forward 1.00–0.97c pm<br><br>The three-month forward quote is, therefore: $1.1455–$1.1548.</div>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	forward rate discount	<div>spot&nbsp; 1.1555–1.1645</div><div>three-month forward 0.79–0.82c dis<br>three-month forward $1.1634–$1.1727<br>discount is used when the dollar is going to be cheaper relative to sterling in the future.&nbsp;<br><br>£1 will buy more dollars in three months’ time and, if you have dollars in three months’ time, the bank will sell you less sterling per dollar than it will now.&nbsp;</div>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	interest rate parity	interest rate parity in determining the exchange rate between currencies arises from one of the cornerstone ideas in financial theory, which is that of rational pricing and the notion of arbitrage.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	arbitrage	Rational pricing is the assumption in financial economics that asset prices will reflect the arbitrage-free price of the asset, as any deviation from this price will be ‘arbitraged away’.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	arbitrage	risk free profit by buying and selling securities in two diff markets when prices are different	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	forward exchange contact vs forward exchange rate&nbsp;	an agreement between two parties to either buy or sell foreign currency at a fixed exchange rate for settlement at a future date.&nbsp;<br><br>The forward exchange rate is the exchange rate set today even though the transaction will not settle until some agreed point in the future, such as in three months’ time.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	relationship between the spot exchange rate and forward exchange rate&nbsp;	given by the differential between their respective nominal interest rates over the term being considered. The relationship is purely mathematical and has nothing to do with market expectations.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	interest rate parity formula for forward rate for GBP/USD	Forward rate for GBP/USD = £ Spot rate x [(1+US $ short term interest rate) / (1+UK £ short term interest rate)]	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	example of interest rate parity	The GBP/USD spot exchange rate = 1.5220. If the three-month interest rate for the UK is 4.88% and for the US, 3.20%, what will the three-month forward exchange rate be?<br><br><div><br></div><div>As the three-month interest rates are quoted on a per annum basis, they must be divided by four to obtain the rate of interest that will be payable (%) over three months:<br><div><br></div><div>Sterling: 4.88% / 4 = 1.22%<br></div><div><div>Dollar:&nbsp; &nbsp; 3.20% / 4 = 0.8%</div></div><br></div>Forward rate for GBP/USD = $1.5220 x [(1+0.008) / (1+0.0122)] = $1.5157	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	Bretton Woods agreement&nbsp;	fixed exchange rates tied to the value of gold	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	end of fixed exchange rates	fixed exchange rates came to an end during the 1970s, largely as a result of a currency crisis for the US dollar and the end of the official convertibility of currencies into gold, which was abandoned in August 1971.&nbsp;<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	pegged currencies	not completely floating - value pegged to another currency<br><br>Middle Eastern countries, such as Saudi Arabia and the UAE, peg their currencies to the US dollar, China pegs its currency to a basket of other currencies, including the US dollar, and the CFA franc in Africa is pegged to the euro. A stricter version of a peg is a currency board, where there are reserve requirements to ensure the peg holds.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	purchasing power parity	concerns the rate to which exchange rates should tend to move over the long term.&nbsp;<br><br>predicts that amounts of different currencies (at current exchange rates) should have equal purchasing power.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	uses of PPP	one economy consistently has an inflation rate in excess of its competitors, then its currency will deteriorate against its trading partners. PPP has also been used to more accurately compare the GDPs of different countries which some believe give a more accurate reflection of the real economies.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	carry trade	borrowing of funds in a currency where the rate of interest is relatively low – examples are the Japanese yen and the Swiss franc – and then the purchase of securities, often government bonds, which have a relatively high yield, such as short-term instruments available from the Australian government.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	what effects demand for US dollars	Overseas operators needing US dollars to pay for imports of US goods to overseas markets now or in the future.<br><br>people wanting to invest in America - need dollars<br><br>if the US dollar is expected to increase relative to one or more other currencies, speculators will buy US dollars ahead of the increase.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	factors effecting the supply of the dollar	When US importers purchase overseas currencies to pay for imported goods arriving in the US, they are increasing the supply of US dollars into the markets.<div><br>US residents wishing to invest in overseas assets will have to sell US dollars to buy overseas currency.<br><br>Supply will be upward-sloping with respect to price.</div>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	nominal amounts traded in FX daily	approximately $7.5 trillion in nominal amounts traded daily.&nbsp;<br><br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	most widely traded currency pair	According to the BIS, the most widely traded currency pair is the USD/EUR, which represents just under one quarter of all trades.&nbsp;<br><br>great depth of trading and, therefore, narrow spreads between buying and selling prices for the commonly quoted and frequently traded currencies like the EUR/USD.&nbsp;<br><br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	example of CEM	currency pairs, however, involving more exotic or less-traded currencies such as the Hungarian forint, will be much less liquid, resulting in much wider spreads between the bid and ask prices.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	what can effect the FX market	releases of inflation data, gross domestic product (GDP) data, industrial production and retail sales data from major government organisations, such as the Office for National Statistics (ONS) in the UK and Eurostat which provides economic data for the EU.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	cryptocurrency	from of digital currency that works independently from a central bank, operating on a blockchain and ledger	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	coin	currency with its own blockchain	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	token	digital asset based on 3rd party blockchain	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	volatility risk in cryptocurrencies&nbsp;	the value of cryptocurrencies to fluctuate by hundreds, or even thousands, of US dollars. Volatility can be driven by many factors including varying perceptions of its intrinsic value as a store of value, financial problems at crypto exchanges, unexpected changes in market sentiment or regulation, and security breaches.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	decentralisation risk to cryptocurrencies	investors deal directly with each other, rather than operating within a centralised exchange. While this allows users to eliminate counterparty risk linked to a centralised exchange, volumes and liquidity are low.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	risk of crypto being unsecured	Cryptocurrencies are not reserve-backed. While traditional currencies are backed by the government that issued them, cryptocurrencies are typically not backed by any tangible assets.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	collective investment	collective investment is a way of investing money alongside other people to participate in a wider range of investments than those feasible for most individual investors, and to share the costs of doing so.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	types of collective investment vehicles	investment funds, managed funds, mutual funds or simply funds. Across the world, large markets have developed around collective investment, and these account for a substantial portion of all trading on major stock exchanges.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	definition of a CIS	The definition of a CIS as given by the International Organization of Securities Commissions (IOSCO), and which is used by many international organisations, is that a CIS is an instrument that:invests in transferable securitiesis publicly marketed, andis open-ended.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	regulated CISs	either authorised by the local financial regulator or, if they are from outside the country, are recognised by the local financial regulator. Recognition generally enables overseas CISs to be marketed to the public in the country&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	unregulated CISs	schemes that are not authorised or recognised by the local financial regulator.<br><br>described as unregulated because they are not subject to the same restrictions as regulated CISs (e.g., in terms of who their potential clients can be and what they invest in). Schemes that are not authorised or recognised are subject to marketing restrictions, - cant be marketed to retail investors	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	risk of unregulated collective investment schemes	client investing in a collective investment scheme could lose some or all of their principal investment.&nbsp;<br>UCISs are generally considered to be a high-risk investment and firms should always ensure that clients fully understand, and are financially able to assume, the associated risks before investing.<br>invest in more complex products than CIS<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	gearing	means that the fund can borrow money to enhance the total funds available to it for investment. If the fund goes up in value (and the capital value of the loan remains static) this can lead to a higher multiple of gains&nbsp;<br><br>risk is that in the event of a loss in the value of the fund’s assets, this can lead to a higher multiple of loss to the investor, because the loan will still require repayment&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	open ended fund	open-ended fund is an investment fund that can issue and redeem shares or units in the scheme at any time.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	how can you invest in an open ended fund	If investors wish to invest in an open-ended fund, they approach the fund directly and provide the money they wish to invest. The fund can create new shares or units in response to this demand, issuing new shares or units to the investor at a price based on the value of the underlying portfolio.&nbsp;<br><br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	what makes a fund open ended	expand and contract in size based on investor demand,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	closed end investment company	When a closed-ended investment company is first established, a set number of shares are issued to the investing public, and these shares are then subsequently traded on a stock market.&nbsp;<br><br>The capital of the fund is, therefore, fixed, and does not expand or contract in the way that an open-ended fund’s capital does.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	investment trusts	company which invests in a diversified range of products. structed as a company not a trust	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	unit trusts	A unit trust is a professionally-managed collective investment fund.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	features of a unit trust	Investors can buy units, each of which represents a specified fraction of the trust.<br>The trust holds a portfolio of securities.<br>The assets of the trust are held by trustees and are invested by manager's.<br>The investor incurs annual management charges and possibly also an initial charge.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	authorised unit trust&nbsp;	a unit trust that is allowed to be marketed to the investing public, and must be constituted by a trust deed made between the manager and the trustee.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	role of trustees in a unit trust	primary duty of the trustees is to protect the interests of the unitholders.&nbsp;<br><br>investors in a unit trust own the underlying value of securities held by the unit trust based on the proportion of the units held. They are effectively the beneficiaries of the trust.&nbsp;<br><br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	requirements for a trustee	Trustees of a unit trust must be authorised by the FCA and be fully independent of the trust manager.<br>Trustees are required to have capital in excess of £4 million and, for this reason, are normally large financial institutions, such as banks or insurance companies.<br><br>The trust deed of each unit trust must clearly state its investment strategy and objectives, so that investors can determine the suitability of each trust.<br><br>The limits and allowable investment areas for a unit trust fund are also laid out in the trust deed together with the investment objectives.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	role of the manager&nbsp; - unit trust	the manager of an AUT must also be authorised by the FCA.&nbsp;<br><br>their jobs are:<br>marketing the unit trust<br>managing the assets in accordance with the trust deed<br>maintaining a record of units for inspection by the trustees<br>supplying other information relating to the investments under the unit trust as requested, and<br>informing the FCA of any breaches of regulations while the manager is running the trust.<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	AUTs buying and selling	AUTs can be purchased in a number of ways; for example, via a newspaper advertisement, over the phone or over the internet. These methods will generally require payment with the order, or some form of guarantee of payment.<br><br>&nbsp;A contract note will be produced and sent to the investor as evidence of the purchase. Investors can sell their units via the same source that they purchased them, or can contact the fund managers direct, for example, electronically.<br><br>different from investment trusts, which are marketed as shares in a company<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	unit trust pricing	calculation of buying and selling prices will take place at the valuation point, which is at a particular time each day.&nbsp;<br><br>The fund is valued on the basis of the net value of the constituent assets and a typical spread between buying and selling prices in the market will be in the range of 5%–7%.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	charges in unit trust	initial charge - charge to buy in<br><br>exit charged - when investor sells<br><br>annual management charge - percentage of value of the underlying fund.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	open ended investment companies	OEICs<br><br>open-ended collective investment formed as a corporation under the Open-Ended Investment Companies Regulations of the United Kingdom.&nbsp;<br>A share in an OEIC entitles the holder to a share of the profits of the OEIC, and the value of the share will be determined by the value of the underlying investments.&nbsp;<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	OEIC features	Holders of shares in an OEIC can sell them back to the company, resulting in the number of shares issued by the OEIC reducing. Similarly, new or existing investors can buy new shares from the OEIC, resulting in the number of shares issued by the OEIC increasing. In both cases, the price at which the deal is done is based on the NAV of the shares.<br><br>OEIC may take the form of an umbrella fund with a number of separately priced sub-funds, adopting different investment strategies&nbsp;
