CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	investment trusts	Investment trusts began in the UK, and the Foreign &amp; Colonial Investment Trust was the first to be founded in 1868 with the aim of ‘giving the investor of moderate means the same advantage as the large capitalist’.&nbsp;<br>provide a way for an investor with relatively modest amounts to invest to have exposure to large portfolios of assets, primarily equities, which would be impractical and prohibitively expensive otherwise.<br><br><br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	how re investment trusts managed	Investment trusts are managed by professional fund managers who select and manage the stocks in the trust’s portfolio.&nbsp;<br><br>accessible to the individual investor, although shares in investment trusts are also widely held by institutional investors, such as pension funds.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	structure of investment trusts	investment trusts are not trusts – they are companies.&nbsp;<br><br>whereas other companies may make their profit from providing goods and services, an investment trust makes its profit solely from investments.&nbsp;<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	how are investment trusts traded	The quotation of the price of investment trust shares is similar to that for equities generally, and a dealer will give two prices:<br><br>The higher price is the offer price, at which an investor can buy the shares.<br>The lower price is the bid price, at which a holder of the shares can sell.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	offer price	the higher of the two prices quoted by a market maker - how much they will buy for.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	bid price,&nbsp;	The lower price is the bid price, at which a holder of the shares can sell.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	NAV	The NAV is essentially the net worth of an investment trust company’s equity capital and is calculated from adding together the following:<br><br>the value of the trust’s listed investments at mid-market prices<br>the value of its unlisted investments at the directors’ valuation, and<br>cash and other net current assets.<br><br>company’s liabilities are deducted from this figure, including any issued preference capital at nominal value.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	what is nav used for	fair value of shares,&nbsp;Most investment trust shares trade at a discount to NAV. The level of discount (or premium) relates to the demand for shares, and any factor that influences demand will affect it.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	diff between open ended and closed ended funds	closed-ended investment trusts have wider investment freedom than authorised open-ended investment funds, such as unit trusts and OEICs.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	what can closed ended funds do	invest in unquoted private companies as well as quoted companies<br>provide venture capital to new companies or companies requiring new funds for expansion, and<br>borrow money to help them achieve their objectives. <br>Authorised open-ended collective schemes’ powers to borrow are more limited. <br>The ability to borrow allows an investment trust to leverage returns for the investor. Such gearing also increases the volatility of returns.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	ETFs	<b>investment funds that are similar to both typical company shares and CISs. They resemble shares in that they trade in real time on stock markets, and are like CISs in that they contain a basket of investments, such as equities or bonds, that are bought and sold by a manager. ETFs are generally passively managed.</b>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	who can buy ETFs from the fund manager	Only authorised participants (typically large institutional investors) actually buy or sell shares of an ETF directly from/to the fund manager,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	creation/redemption units	large blocks of tens of thousands of ETF shares&nbsp;<br>usually exchanged in kind with baskets of the underlying securities.&nbsp;<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	APs	authorised participants, able to buy/sell ETFs from source<br><br>Authorised participants may wish to invest in the ETF shares long-term, but usually act as market makers on the open market, using their ability to exchange creation/redemption units with their underlying securities to provide liquidity of the ETF shares&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	why are ETFs preffered over mutsal funds	Investors prefer ETFs to traditional funds because they are easier to trade, have lower operating costs, and are sometimes tax advantaged.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	trading of ETFs	pricing takes place in real time and the funds can be bought or sold anytime the markets are open.&nbsp;<br><br>unlike the position with many types of collective investment vehicles, such as unit trusts and other investment funds, where the pricing is only computed at the end of each day.&nbsp;<br><br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	who runs ETFs	Many ETF products are managed by large financial intermediaries, including BlackRock, State Street, Vanguard and JP Morgan Chase, and the fees and charges are very competitive and often considerably lower than those applied for more traditionally managed funds.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	psychical replication - ETF tracking	the ETF will buy and own most or all of an index’s constituents in order to replicate the index’s performance. Despite being simple and transparent, since physical replication involves buying and selling index components, this strategy is inherently labour-intensive and costly.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	synthetic replication - ETFs	synthetic replication, typically, involves the ETF provider entering into a contract with a counterparty (typically a bank) to deliver the return of the fund’s benchmark index in exchange for a fee.&nbsp;<br><br>This swap contract means that, for example, an equity ETF will not actually hold any stocks at all. Instead, it will have a contractual relationship with the counterparty bank.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	Exchange traded notes	<b>trade similarly to ETFs and also often track indices, but are created differently. These are structured investment products and are senior debt notes typically issued by major banks.&nbsp;<br><br>also have counterpaaty risk</b>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	ETF derivatves	Some ETFs hold derivatives and offer large amounts of leverage, attempting to track the underlying index returns at a ratio of 2x or even 3x.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	specialist funds	These vehicles essentially turn illiquid assets, or assets that investors would find difficult to invest in, into liquid investments that are available to institutional and retail investors.&nbsp;<br><br>such as private equity, property or infrastructure.&nbsp; (private credit funds)	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	type of investment - specialist funds	long-term investment strategies and so the closed-end nature of the structure is more appropriate to their needs. Closed-ended funds have the advantage that the fund does not need to realise assets at possibly distressed prices to meet investor redemptions.&nbsp;<br><br>they may require investors to commit their money for long periods of time so that the planned investments have time to deliver their expected returns.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	private equity	Private equity investment is usually structured as a limited partnership arrangement and looks to target institutional investors and only the wealthiest private investors.&nbsp;<br><br>Private equity firms invest in other businesses and then try to maximise their returns by exiting the company at a profit.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	how do PE firms make money	Private equity funds make money by investing in businesses that need access to growth capital or that need overhauling. The types and spread of investments they hold need to be analysed, along with any commitments they may have to provide further capital.<br><br>They will often use high levels of debt and so are exposed to interest rate rises and credit squeezes. Funds will look to take their profit by a trade sale or flotation and so their planned exit route needs evaluation.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	structured products	investments which provide a return based on the performance of an underlying asset, such as an equity index.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	who issues structured products	issued by banks and insurance companies,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	who are structured products designed for	designed for investors who are prepared to invest for a fixed period, and who also want some degree of protection over their initial capital.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	features of structured products	They either offer income or growth.<br>They have defined returns and defined risks.<br>Returns are linked to one or more defined external measures, such as the S&amp;P 500 or any other equities index, gold prices and oil prices.<br>They run for a defined term, typically at least 18 months, but not more than seven years.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	2 subcategories of structured products	structured deposits/structured investments	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	structured deposits	cash-based products and can only be offered by banks which are able to accept deposits.&nbsp;<br><br>designed to return at least the initial amount deposited at the end of the product’s life.&nbsp;<br><br>differs from structured investments as the underlying asset is cash based, therefore protected by FSCS	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	structured investments	sub-divided into principal-protected investment products and capital at risk investment products&nbsp;<br><br>pre-packaged investment based on a number of asset classes.&nbsp;primarily, innovations designed to fulfil customised return/risk objectives.&nbsp;&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	principal gurantee	feature of some structured products&nbsp;<br><br>principal guarantee which offers protection of the principal, if held to maturity. These capital-protected structured products are designed to return the original capital even if the underlying linked measure underperforms.&nbsp;<br><br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	sCaRPs	Structured capital at risk products	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	soft floor	the investor will get most or all of their money back as long as the underlying index does not fall by more than a certain proportion. If the index does fall by more than the ‘soft floor’, the investor will suffer the full loss.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	precipice bond	an investment that will pay an attractive level of income over a set period, eg, 10% per annum over three years. However, the ‘precipice’ is that if a reference index, such as a stock market index, falls by more than a certain level over that period, the capital will suffer an equivalent loss.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	why are structured products used	Structured products are created to meet the specific needs of high net worth individuals (HNWIs) and general retail investors that cannot be met by standardised financial instruments available in the markets.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	how are structured products made	combining underlying assets, such as bonds, with derivatives, that might be based on shares, indices, currencies or commodities. This combination can create structures that have significant risk/return and cost-saving advantages compared to what might otherwise be obtainable in the market,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	structured deposits payout example	The rules for a structured deposit lasting three years might be as follows for an investment of $1,000:<br><br>If the S&amp;P 500 is higher at the end of the three years than it was at the beginning, the depositor gets the original investment back plus interest equal to 100% of the growth in the index.<br><br>If the S&amp;P 500 is lower or the same at the end of three years than it was at the beginning, the depositor only gets the original investment back.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	callable structured products	the product will mature early as soon as the underlying asset&nbsp;breaches a certain level on a pre-defined observation date. This is often described as the investor being ‘kicked out’ and receiving the initial investment and (potentially) a return.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	‘range accruals pay-off’. - structured products	a pay-off that is based on the underlying asset remaining within a certain range on set dates.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	Averaging value structured products.&nbsp;	structured products that determine the return based on the average level of the underlying asset over the life of the product rather than the value at maturity.&nbsp;<br><br>useful in that it protects the investor against a temporary, short-term fall in the underlying around maturity, but can also mean that the full gain in the value of the underlying might not be realised.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	"structured products - ""lookback"" feature"	gives the investor the ability to realise the returns based on the highest percentage rise of the underlying over the period of the structured product, rather than the maturity value or the average value.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	Cash or nothing pay-off structured products&nbsp;	either paid or not, depending on a particular event linked to the reference underlying asset.&nbsp;<br><br>a product with payment of the investor’s capital at maturity and a fixed payment if the underlying is above the target level (usually the initial level) at maturity. If this does not occur, then only the capital is repaid.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	Quantity adjusting (quantos) structured products&nbsp;	designed to protect the investor against adverse currency movements when the underlying is denominated in a foreign currency.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	counterparty risk - structured products	possibility of the counterparty to the investor collapsing.&nbsp;<br><br>product is a structured deposit, there is the potential of a deposit protection scheme for the banking industry which provides protection in the event of a particular bank collapsing. However, for structured investment products, there will be no such safety net.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	credit risk - structured products	the protection of the principal will depend upon the creditworthiness of the counterparty providing the protection guarantee. <br>Even though the cash flows and the principal in a structured product may come from a stable investment (eg, a bond), the products themselves are legally considered to be the issuer’s liability.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	example of structured products risk	as the products are issued by investment banks - its their risk and credit rating that matters more - if the issuing bank goes under payout for the structured product wont happen even if the product is AAA rated.<br><br>Several firms that issued structured products (including Lehman Brothers) went into administration or bankruptcy during the financial crisis and could not meet their obligations. Investors in structured products had to wait, along with bondholders, to recover their funds invested with the banks who offered such products.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	structured product example	EUR five-year Bitcoin-Gold Switcher Note.&nbsp;<br><br>The investor receives 6% pa in euros and at maturity receives the principal (€100 face value per note) plus the higher of double the return on Bitcoin or gold through to the end of the five-year period. If losses have been incurred, the smallest loss will be doubled and deducted from the principal up to the full amount, so possibly 100% of the face value.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	summary of structured products	Structured investment products can offer extremely customised risk-return payoffs and access to exposure in many assets not offered by traditional securities markets, but the many disadvantages or specific risks must be carefully considered to determine suitability.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	other issues with structured products	fees payable for these products will be higher than for many simpler products or via holding the assets directly.<br>structured products may utilise a complex set of instruments or illiquid derivatives.&nbsp;<br><br>product will have to be held to maturity to secure any gains, unless it is a listed structured product.&nbsp;<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 3: MARKETS	2 types of markets	the primary and secondary markets.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 3: MARKETS	what is a stock exchange	organised marketplaces for issuing securities and then trading those securities via their members. All stock exchanges provide both a primary and a secondary market.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 3: MARKETS	primary market	the new issues market, facilitates new issues of securities.&nbsp;<br><br>The primary markets exist to enable issuers of securities to raise capita and to enable the surplus funds held by potential investors to be matched with investment opportunities the issuers offer.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 3: MARKETS	term for raising capital on stock exchange	they access the primary market and float (or ‘list’).&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 3: MARKETS	initial public offering	IPO - new issue of ordinary shares, company gaining a sock market listing for the first time.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 3: MARKETS	how do companies achieve flotation on the stock exchange	offers for investors to subscribe for their shares (offers for subscription), or sometimes raising no money at all, called ‘listing by way of introduction'.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 3: MARKETS	the secondary market	secondary market is where existing securities are traded between investors.&nbsp;<br><br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 3: MARKETS	who provides systems for secondary market trading	Stock exchanges and other trading venues, such as multilateral trading facilities (MTFs), provide systems to assist in this.&nbsp;<br><br>These systems provide investors with liquidity, giving them the ability to sell their securities if they wish.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 3: MARKETS	how does secondary market trading work	Trading activity in the secondary market also results in the ongoing provision of buy and sell prices ('bid/ask') to investors via the exchange’s member firms of stockbrokers and investment banks.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 3: MARKETS	users of primary markets	Issuers of new securities, such as corporations engaging in IPOs or follow-on offerings, as well as the issuers of certain kinds of debt instruments, are the main suppliers of new securities in the primary markets.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 3: MARKETS	main purchasers of newly issued securities	large institutional investors, such as pension funds, insurance companies and collective investment vehicles, including exchange-traded funds (ETFs) and mutual funds, who buy the securities being offered on behalf of their clients, who are primarily members of the general public.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 3: MARKETS	users of the secondary market	used by short-term speculators and traders who may hold securities for very short periods&nbsp;<br><br>large institutional investors, such as pension funds, insurance companies and collective investment vehicles, including exchange-traded funds (ETFs) and mutual funds&nbsp;will be engaged in selling previously owned securities within their portfolio and, in turn, adding other securities available on the secondary market.&nbsp;<br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 3: MARKETS	how can retail traders access secondary markets	through brokerages, including robo-advisors or via direct investing platforms. Both buyers and sellers of existing shares, ETFs and debt instruments trade in the secondary market.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 3: MARKETS	principal users of secondary markets	various funds and trading vehicles which engage in automated trading strategies that are triggered by computer-programmed algorithms.&nbsp;<br><br>objective is to exploit transitory price discrepancies requiring rapid trade execution, such strategies are referred to as high-frequency trading (HFT).	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 3: MARKETS	motivation behind primary markets	to raise capital by accessing the substantial market made up of public market investors.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 3: MARKETS	why do share issuers want to raise capital	business expansion or acquisitions,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 3: MARKETS	why do buyers of assets participate in primary markets	buyers of the offerings may want to allocate their capital across what they perceive as attractive investment opportunities.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 3: MARKETS	flipping	buying into an IPO which is price inflated by excitement, then selling very quickly afterwards	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 3: MARKETS	The principal use and benefit of secondary markets&nbsp;	they provide a liquid environment within which owners of securities can sell a current holding and where willing buyers can purchase existing securities.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 3: MARKETS	capitalisation issue	bonus/scrip issue	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 3: MARKETS	liquid markets	lots of assets available - tighter bid/ask spreads	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 3: MARKETS	characteristic of a liquid market	bid/ask spreads are narrow. Another feature which provides for more liquidity is the <br><br>activity of speculators, continuous buying and selling of securities with very short-term holding period horizons provides a depth to the secondary market, which would not be available if the secondary market only existed for institutions seeking longer-term investment reallocations.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 3: MARKETS	a listing	company who's securities are listed to be available to trade on the stock market	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 3: MARKETS	UK regulator	Financial Conduct Authority (FCA) created the UK listing rules - book of rules&nbsp;which details companies’ shares and bonds (including UK government bonds or gilts) can be admitted to be traded on the London Stock Exchange (LSE).&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 3: MARKETS	FCA/LSE relationship	FCA sets the rules relating to becoming listed on the LSE. The LSE is itself responsible for the operation of the exchange, including the trading of the securities on the secondary market, although the FCA can suspend the listing of particular securities and, therefore, remove their secondary market trading activity on the exchange.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 3: MARKETS	US regulator	SEC -&nbsp;requires companies seeking a listing on the US exchanges, such as the NYSE and Nasdaq, to register and provide certain details to the SEC first.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 3: MARKETS	Chinese regulator	China Securities Regulatory Commission (CSRC),&nbsp;<br><br>in China the exchanges themselves&nbsp;(the Shanghai, Shenzhen and Beijing stock exchanges) have a lot of authority over new issues	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	example of a eurobond	bond issued in france, in US dollars	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	settlement period of US gov bonds	T+1	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	maturity period for US T-bonds	over 10 years	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	legal owner of the underlying shares in an ADR	the depositary bank	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	PV calculation	coupon payment / 1+IR + ((coupon payment + nominal return)/1+IR^2)	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	advantage of ZCBs	sold at less than nominal value	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	how does DMO standing repo facility help improve liquidity in GILTS	allows GEMMs to enter into a reverse repo to cover shorts	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	how is the reference RPI figure derived for index linked gilts	RPI figures used to calculate the needed inflation adjustment are those from 3 months before coupon payment and 3 months before issue date	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	difference between covered and conventional warrants	covered warrants issued by 3rd parties not the company itself	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	which US security is a ZCB	treasury bill	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	money market instrument which requires high quality collateral	repo	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	owners prior to an IPO	owners prior to the IPO are likely to include the founders of the company and/or relatives or close acquaintances and early-stage investors, such as private equity (PE) or venture capital (VC) funds.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	advantages of IPO	IPOs can raise substantial sums of capital and create a great deal of publicity for the issuing companies.&nbsp;<br><br>money raised is equity - no debt taken on	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	underwriting an IPO	when financial institutions agree to buy the securities being issued if demand is insufficient	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	Special Purpose Acquisition Companies&nbsp;	Special Purpose Acquisition Companies. They are alternatively referred to as ‘blank cheque companies’.<br><br>listed companies that are usually established with a two-year life span and the sole purpose of effecting a merger with a private company to enable that private company to go public.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	SPAC	listed companies that are usually established with a two-year life span and the sole purpose of effecting a merger with a private company to enable that private company to go public.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	how is a SPAC created	management team, known as the sponsor, set up the SPAC and invest enough cash to cover the initial operating expenses&nbsp;<br><br>In return for the investment the sponsors get ‘founder shares’ which will often be around 20% of the SPAC.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	how does a SPAC raise external capital	The team assembled by the sponsors will raise capital from investors – typically in the hundreds of $ millions. If there is no merger consummated in the time-span of the SPAC, this investment will be redeemed (potentially with interest added).<br><br>investors are also given the opportunity to redeem their shares if they dislike the merger target and/or the terms of the merger&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	how does a SPAC trade	taken public and listed on an appropriate exchange. It is typical for the outside investors to be given both shares and warrants at this stage. The US standard is that the shares cost $10 each and the warrants are exercisable at $11.50. The warrants can be traded separately.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	maximum lifespan of SPAC	after a SPAC is public, it has 2 years to find a private company to take public before it expires<br>The SPAC has a maximum of two years to find and agree terms with its target.<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	bear market	conventially defined as a 20% drop in markets, but its ust used to describe when markets are down or bearish	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	follow-on offering.&nbsp;	A follow-on offering is alternatively referred to as a secondary offer. Issuing more shares in a follow-on offering will only be considered if the equity markets are sufficiently robust.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	how is a follow on offering structured	follow-on offering will be structured with a base number of shares that the company is planning to issue. Again, the issuing company may also retain a greenshoe option to increase the number of shares that it issues, if significant levels of demand would otherwise remain unsatisfied.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	greenshoe option	over allotment option gives the underwriters of an IPO the right to sell additional securities in an offering, if demand for the securities is higher than the original amount offered.<br><br>reserve stock of shares if og stock sells quick&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	stages of a secondary offering	The decision – the issuing company decides to issue additional shares to raise capital through a follow-on offering.<br><br>The preparation of the prospectus – this should be relatively easy, since the issuing company has prepared a prospectus before, when it first became a listed entity.<br><br>The sale of securities – as in an IPO, the appointed investment bank will lead-manage the sale and may well establish a syndicate of co-managers to assist in selling the securities to its clients.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	offers for sale	The company seeking to sell the shares approaches an issuing house (usually an investment bank) that specialises in approaching potential shareholders and preparing the necessary documentation.<br><br>The issuing company sells its shares to the issuing house (usually an investment bank), which then invites applications from the public at a slightly higher price than the issuing house has paid and on the basis of a detailed prospectus, also known as the offer document.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	types of offers for sale	fixed price offer/tender offer	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	fixed price offer	price of the IPO is fixed just below the believed fair value price - to encourage activeness in secondary markets<br><br>If the offer is oversubscribed, as it nearly always is, due to the favourable pricing formula, then shares are allotted either by scaling down each application or by satisfying a randomly chosen proportion of the applications in full.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	tender offer	the issuer does not stipulate a fixed price for the shares but invites tenders for the issue, usually by setting a minimum tender price. Investors state the number of shares they wish to purchase and state the price per share they are prepared to pay.<br><br>avoids being oversubscribed - auction	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	over allotment options	gives the underwriters of an IPO the right to sell up to an additional 15% of the original number of shares at the IPO price in a registered securities offering, if demand outstrips supply	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	placing shares	company markets shares to a specific broker/bank - who allots them with specific clients	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	when can placing be used	can be used for IPOs but also for secondary (or follow-on) issues. If the company is based in a jurisdiction that gives shareholders pre-emptive rights, a resolution at a shareholders’ meeting will be required to enable the placing to happen.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	another name for placing	selective marketing	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	private placements	accredited investors where shares are marketed to them without the need of a regular prospectus	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	direct listing	direct listings create demand at the time of the initial trading on the exchange by the efforts of the company itself.&nbsp;<br><br>no underwriters/banks for the IPO.<br><br>The trading price will be set based on actual demand at the time of listing, with neither the company nor the financial advisers engaging in stabilisation activities.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	why would direct listing be used over traditional IPOs	potential listing mechanisms for companies that do not need to raise capital, perhaps&nbsp; because the company is already cash flow positive.&nbsp;<br><br>direct listing is a company that is already well-understood, meaning it does not require investment banks to educate the investor base in terms of what it does, how successful it is and its future opportunities.&nbsp;<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	introduction	a company applying for or gaining a listing for its shares without raising funds&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	why is an introduction unusual&nbsp;	most companies use listing as an opportunity to raise extra funds and some companies are forced to issue more shares to comply with regulations that typically require a minimum percentage of shares to be held by people other than the founders and other significant shareholders.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	why would an introduction be used	introduction is used by a company that does not need to raise extra capital through share issues, but wishes to gain the extra liquidity in its shares that a listing provides.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	Exchangeable bonds and convertible bonds&nbsp;	hybrid instruments, with characteristics of both equities and bonds.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	convertible bond&nbsp;	a bond&nbsp;paying a coupon and with a nominal value to be repaid on maturity, offering the holder of the bond the right to convert the bond into a set number of ordinary shares of the company that issued the bond.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	exchangeable bond&nbsp;	bond that pays a coupon and has a set redemption date. Like a convertible, it gives the holder the right to exchange the bond for a set number of shares, but these shares are not those of the bond issuer, but of another company’s shares that are held by the issuer.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	benefits of holding&nbsp;convertible or an exchangeable bond&nbsp;	safety of coupons and repayment, combined with the potential upside of equity growth.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	benefits&nbsp;convertible or an exchangeable bond to issuer	Both types of bond enable the issuer to raise borrowed funds more cheaply, because the bonds have the upside potential of the conversion/ exchange into shares.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	how are&nbsp;convertible or an exchangeable bonds issued	structure of an offering of a convertible or exchangeable bond mirrors that of equities – the issuer will set a base amount of bonds it wishes to issue and perhaps retain a greenshoe, reserving the right to issue more if demand is strong.<br><br>marketed like an IPO	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	flotation on stock market benefits	company can gain access to capital and enable its shares to be readily marketable are often-quoted positives. Further, listing on an exchange could help with the company’s brand awareness and ultimate profitability.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	flotation on stock market drawbacks	the fact that the original owners may lose control of the company and that the ongoing disclosure and reporting requirements required by the company after listing&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	listing agent	alternatively referred to as a sponsor. The sponsor is likely to be an investment bank, a stockbroking firm or a professional services firm such as an accountancy practice.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	role of a sponsor - IPO	role of the sponsor includes assessing the company’s suitability for listing, the best method of bringing the company to the market, and coordinating the production of the prospectus.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	what is the prospectus - IPO	detailed document about the company, including financial information that should enable prospective investors to decide on the merits of the company’s shares.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	nominated advisor&nbsp;	the listing agent or sponsor on the LSE’s AIM,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	The reporting accountants jobs - IPO	will attest to the validity of the financial information provided in the prospectus.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	legal advisors jobs - IPO	legal advisers will make sure that all relevant matters are covered in the prospectus and that the statements made are justified.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	due diligence for the prospectus&nbsp;	The combination of the reporting accountants and the legal advisers -&nbsp;making sure the document is accurate and complies with the regulations.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	PR consultant&nbsp;	generally appointed to optimise the positive public perception of the company and its products and services in the run-up to listing.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	origination team&nbsp;	corporate broker/advisory firm, which may be the same firm as the listing agent.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	corporate brokers role	are to act as an interface between the company on the one hand, and the stock market and investors in the company’s securities on the other.&nbsp;<br><br>the corporate broker advises the company on market conditions&nbsp;<br><br>the way existing and potential investors are viewing the company in relation to its peers, and the general direction of the market.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	Corporate governance&nbsp;	the set of laws, rules, customs and both external and internal policies that guide how a company is directed and managed.&nbsp;<br>it is expected&nbsp;that the listed companies have put in place appropriate corporate and management structures.&nbsp;<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	examples of corporate governance rules	reducing the influence of a single individual by splitting the roles of chairman and chief executive of the company, appointing a reasonable proportion of non-executive directors (NEDs) to the board, and having a suitably qualified finance director.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	Reporting requirements use	designed to make sure that existing and potential investors are kept informed of progress and developments at the listed company. It is particularly important that financial information is provided regularly and that the information is reliable, and so listed companies are generally required to provide audited annual accounts; and less detailed half-yearly, or perhaps quarterly, reports.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	Deal Syndication	when a company is planning a large IPO -the sponsor (bank) will gather a syndicate group of investment banks and brokers to market the share issue to their clients.&nbsp;<br><br>the issue may be large enough to warrant the appointment of more than one lead manager, often described as co-leads. Each co-lead may be responsible for a particular geographical area – for example, one co-lead for Europe, another for the US.<br><br>the syndicate and the issuer agree on a price range for the offering. The difference between the upper and lower values of the price range may be as high as 10 to 15 per cent.<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	book building	finding buyers in an issue of securities, such as company shares and an IPO	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	Underwriting&nbsp;	agreeing with financial institutions, such as banks, insurance companies and asset managers, that, if the demand is insufficient, the financial institutions will buy the shares.&nbsp;<br><br>insurance policy that the issue will happen because, in the worst case, the underwriters (the financial institutions that have agreed to underwrite the offer) will buy the shares.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	Stabilisation&nbsp;	to prevent a substantial fall in the value of securities when a large number of new securities are issued, the lead manager of the issue agrees to support the price by buying back the newly issued securities in the market if the market price falls below a certain predefined level.&nbsp;<br><br>done in an attempt to give the market a reasonable chance to adjust to the increased number of securities that have become available,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	why is stabilisation used	attempt to give the market a reasonable chance to adjust to the increased number of securities that have become available, by stabilising the price at which they are traded.<br><br>By increasing the demand for the securities in the market at the same time as more securities become available, the price should remain more stable. This will mean the issuing company’s securities appear less volatile, and existing investors will be less likely to begin panic-selling,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	how is stabilisation used	The typical method used to stabilise the price of shares after an IPO is to use a greenshoe option, issuing more shares if there is sufficient demand, or limiting the supply by not exercising the greenshoe if buying is lacklustre.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	FCA rules on stabilisation	FCA requires disclosure to the market that stabilisation is happening, and that the market price may not be a representative one because of the stabilisation activities. Prices can also be stabilised by exchanges using circuit breakers to temporarily suspend trading in periods of volatility.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	circuit breakers	automated suspension of trading when prices rises or fall too much	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 2: ASSET CLASSES	which US government security is a zero coupon bond	treasury bill	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	role of stock exchanges	facilitate the secondary market trading of listed securities.&nbsp;<br><br>Buyers and sellers trade with one another over the exchange, and supply and demand for the listed securities determines the price.&nbsp;<br><br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	what investments are traded on exchange	Investments traded on exchanges can include common or preference shares, ETFs, bonds and options.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	participants in an investment exchange	Trades are arranged by stockbrokers (brokers) and the arranged trades will often involve purchasing from, or selling to, a bank which is acting as a dealer or, in some cases, a market maker.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	3 strands of regulatory framework - stock exchanges	the law, the requirements of the local regulator and the rules of the stock exchange itself.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	first stage of listing shares with regulator	filing of a prospectus with the regulator that contains information about the securities’ issuer, its history, financial situation and its operations.&nbsp;<br><br>coordinated by a sponsor,&nbsp;supported by reporting accountants vetting the numbers and legal advisers ensuring the sponsor has discharged its legal responsibilities.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	stage 2 - listing securities with the regulator	application to the stock exchange to have the securities listed. The exchange will want to see that there is a minimum number of shares held by persons other than the issuing company’s directors and other dominant investors&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	AIM market	LSEs market for small cap uk companies - less demanding admission requirements&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	hong kong markets	the senior and junior markets are referred to as the Main Board and the Growth Enterprise Market.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	main market - uk	the LSE -&nbsp;<br><br>The Main Market in the UK is for securities included within the official list and the criteria for admission to the official list are set out in the UK Listing Rules – a rulebook is maintained by the UK regulator – the FCA.

CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	share ownership - going public rules	At least 10% of the company’s shares should be in public hands, or be available for public purchase. The term ‘public’ excludes directors and their associates and anyone who holds 5% or more of the shares.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	obligations of companies which are listed	obliged to issue a half-yearly report in addition to annual accounts, and they have to notify the market of any new, price-sensitive information.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	rules around AIM companies	there is no restriction on market value or percentage of shares in public hands. There is also no minimum period for a company to have been in existence.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	nomad - AIM	important rules for the AIM is the requirement that AIM companies need to have a nominated adviser.&nbsp;<br><br>typically a firm of stockbrokers or accountants or an investment bank. Each nomad needs to be approved by the LSE for assessing the appropriateness of the applicant for AIM, and for guiding and advising AIM companies on their responsibilities under the rules of the market that have been laid down by the LSE.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	requirements for a company’s shares to be admitted to the AIM&nbsp;	That there is no restriction on the transferability of the shares.<br><br>That the AIM company appoints two experts to assist it:<br><br>the nominated adviser – as seen, the nomad can be thought of as an exchange expert, advising the company on all aspects of AIM Listing Rules and compliance<br><br>the broker – AIM companies’ shares are usually less liquid than those of fully listed companies; it is the broker’s job to ensure that there is a market in the company’s shares, to facilitate trading in those shares and to provide ongoing information about the company to interested parties.

CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	what are bonds	IOU (I owe you) instruments that specify a face value, coupon rate and redemption date.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	who issues bonds	Supranationals&nbsp;<br>Governments&nbsp;<br>Municipalities<br>Agencies&nbsp;<br>Corporates&nbsp;<br>Financial institutions and special purpose vehicles (SPVs)	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	what are supernationals	organisations like the World Bank or Asian Development Bank raise money through issuing bonds.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	what are&nbsp;Municipalities	cities in the US issue municipal bonds to finance local borrowing. These municipal bonds are often also tax-efficient for resident investors. Municipal bonds are usually guaranteed by a third party, known in the US market as a monoline insurer, and their credit quality may be enhanced by such a guarantee, which enables the municipality to secure funds on more advantageous terms.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS			
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	(‘Fannie Mae’),&nbsp;	Federal National Mortgage Association -&nbsp;created to provide mortgage finance for the disadvantaged, can issue bonds	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	(‘Sallie Mae’)&nbsp;	Student Loan Marketing Association&nbsp;<br>created to finance student education.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	how is a bond issued	large sums of money were being raised in a single capital-raising transaction.&nbsp;<br><br>details of the bond would be established, including its coupon and maturity, and the bonds would be marketed to potential investors.&nbsp;<br><br>investors would either be invited to bid for the bonds in an auction-type process, or a tender method was adopted.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	‘shelf registration’&nbsp;	enables a single registration to be used for a number of bond issues over a period of up to two years.&nbsp;<br><br>made the bond issuance pipeline easier	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	medium-term note (MTN) market&nbsp;	bonds with generally two to ten years between issue and maturity.&nbsp; - uses lots of shelf registration	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	process of shelf registration	bond issuer finding two or more dealers that are willing to offer their services to market the bonds to their clients on a best efforts basis. The issuer will then issue bonds as and when the money is required<br><br>coupon rates and maturity are changed in accordance with market demand.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	pitching - bond issuance	issuer of the bonds will need to decide that a bond issue is appropriate and which investment bank(s) it wants to assist in the issue.&nbsp;<br><br>This process, with different investment banks competing, is sometimes called a ‘beauty pageant'.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	Indicative bid - bond issuance	during the pitching stage, the banks will detail their views of how much finance the issuer is likely to raise given the terms of the bond issue.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	mandate announcement - bond issuance	once the issuer has decided upon the bank(s) to raise the finance on its behalf, it will announce the names of the banks that have been given the mandate to arrange the issue on its behalf.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	credit rating - bond issuance	issuer of the bond is scrutinised by rating agencies on cash flow and creditworthiness to determine the rating of the bond, which in term determines the yield of the bond and markets its traded on.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	credit enhancements	an insurance policy to heighten the rating of the bond	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	Roadshow - bond issuance	series of visits to the potential buyers of the bonds.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	Listing&nbsp; - bond issuance	if the bond is to be listed it will need a prospectus to submit to the relevant listing authority.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	Syndication - bond issuance	for larger bond issues - when multiple banks are acting for the issuer. these banks will&nbsp;sell into their particular client base, perhaps based on geographical regions. The total of all the banks involved is the syndicate.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	what are trading venues	Trading venues facilitate the purchase and sale of listed instruments, particularly equities, and have traditionally been dominated by national stock exchanges,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	different global stock exchanges	New York Stock Exchange (NYSE) and Nasdaq in the US, the UK’s London Stock Exchange (LSE), Germany’s Frankfurt Stock Exchange (FWB) and Japan’s Tokyo Stock Exchange (TSE).&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	who has membership to stock exchanges	Stock exchanges offer membership to investment banks and firms of stockbrokers.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	what can you do as a member of a stock echange	Becoming a member of an exchange enables these banks and stockbrokers to be involved in secondary market trades.&nbsp;<br><br>these exchanges provide liquidity to existing and potential investors, enabling existing investors to sell their securities and allowing potential investors to become actual investors by purchasing securities.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	role of brokers	Brokers arrange deals for their clients, as well as potentially giving advice to their clients, as to which securities they should buy, sell or retain. In return for arranging (and potentially advising), the brokers will earn a commission that is typically calculated as a set percentage of the value of the deal.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	dealers vs brokers	The broker never actually buys or sells securities, dealers can	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	broker - dealers	exchange member firm that can act as a broker for clients and deal securities on their own account or book	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	alternative trading systems	ATS, platform for trading securities not recognised on exchanges	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	over the counter	OTC trading,&nbsp;refers to buying and selling securities outside of an official stock exchange, typically by dealers trading with one another that is not coordinated or subject to the formal procedures of an exchange.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	dark pool trading	form of ATS with little transparency,&nbsp;private trading system that enables clients to trade anonymously. The price and order size information is not displayed in a dark pool until after the trade is complete.&nbsp;<br><br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	ATS VS MTF	alternative trading system, multilateral trading facility - ATS is American, MTF is UK	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	private transactions	those offerings of securities which are made, not to the general public, but to a subset of so-called sophisticated investors, when the same rigorous kinds of disclosures that have to be made in an initial public offering (IPO) prospectus can be avoided.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	private placement	funding round of securities which are sold without an IPO, and without the formality of an approved prospectus, pitching to VCs/PE firms	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	what can make up private placements	stocks, shares of common stock or preferred stock or other forms of membership interests, warrants or promissory notes&nbsp;<br><br>purchasers are often institutional investors such as banks, insurance companies and pension funds.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	who uses private placements	used by growing businesses in their early stages, such as many technology businesses that require substantial amounts of cash as they develop their business model and scale in advance of an IPO.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	tiers of markets	developed markets (G10), emerging markets (BRICS), frontier markets	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	difference between developed/emerging/frontier	Less developed markets around the world, including those classified as emerging and frontier markets, tend not to have reached the same degree of sophistication as developed markets. Most of the equity trading in these markets takes place through stock exchanges.&nbsp;
