CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	Quote-driven systems	market makers agree to buy and sell at least a set minimum number of shares at quoted prices.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	Order-driven systems	the investors indicate how many securities they want to buy or sell, and at what price.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	two way prices	prices quoted by market maker, bid/ask price	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	‘order book’	The orders that await matching&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	issue with order driven system	order-driven system can lack liquidity, since transactions can only be matched against other orders.<br><br>if there are insufficient orders, trades cannot be matched.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	how does a prime broker provide trade leverage	by trading on a margin	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	discount broker vs full service broker	discount brokers charge less commission as they are execution only	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	how are stock market indexes constructed&nbsp;	constituent companies market capitalisation	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	what can a broker dealing as agent do on a stock exchange	facilitate transactions between two parties, does not buy or sell	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	why has volume of bonds traded on single dealer platforms fallen	banks are less willing to use capital in the bond market	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	settlement of trades prior to a trading holt	settled as normal	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	how are components of a DJIA 30 determined	selected by a company	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	GEMM	Gilt edged market maker	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	GEMM obligations	make two way prices to customers	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	hybrid systems&nbsp;	combine features of both order-driven and quote-driven systems&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	examples of hybrid systems	LSE’s Stock Exchange Electronic Trading Service – quotes and crosses (SETSqx).	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	principal and agent.&nbsp;	principal it is essentially buying shares for its own account,&nbsp;<br><br>agent is executing orders for clients	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	firm acting as an agent	buying shares for its own account, in the hope of the shares increasing in value before it sells them, or, in the case of a short transaction, selling borrowed shares at a higher price at the time of borrowing than the price it has to pay when it wishes to replace or cover.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	firm acting as an agent	arranging and making deals on behalf of other third parties, and it makes money, when acting in this capacity, by charging a commission on the deal.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	broker-dealers.&nbsp;	dual capacity to either arrange deals (acting as a broker), or to buy and sell shares for themselves (acting as a dealer).	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	market maker.&nbsp;	stands ready to provide a source of liquidity to certain sections of the market.<br><br>By being prepared to provide a bid for shares that third parties want to sell and an ask for parties that want to buy shares at any time, the market maker smooths out the more erratic price movements that can occur without this additional source of market liquidity.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	how can a firm become a market maker	must apply to the stock exchange, giving details of the securities in which it has chosen to deal. It must provide prices at which it is willing to buy and sell a minimum number of its chosen shares throughout the course of the trading day.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	order flow for market makers	market makers hope to enjoy the benefits of a steady stream of business, from broker- dealers and from other investors.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	inter-dealer broker&nbsp;	inter-dealer broker (IDB) is an exchange member firm that has registered with the exchange to act as an agent between dealers<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	benefit of using an IBD	When one dealer trades with another, it often prefers its identity to remain a secret. This is the key benefit of using an IDB.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	how does an IDB remain annonymous	The IDB is acting as agent for the dealer but settles any transactions as if it were principal in order to preserve the anonymity of the dealer.&nbsp;<br><br>IDB is not allowed to take principal positions, and it has to be a separate firm, not a division of another broker-dealer.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	Prime brokerage&nbsp;	collection of services provided by investment banks to their hedge fund clients.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	prime brokerage services -&nbsp;Securities lending and borrowing	cover short positions in a hedge fund’s long/short strategy.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	prime brokerage services -&nbsp;Leveraged trade execution	undertaking trades on the fund’s behalf that are partly financed by borrowed funds, essentially providing margin for conducting trades.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	prime brokerage services - cash management	maximising the return that is generated from cash held by the fund.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	prime brokerage services - core settlement	make sure that any securities purchased become the property of the fund, and that the appropriate cash is received for any sales made of the fund’s securities in a timely manner.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	prime brokerage services - custody	keeping safe the securities held by the fund and processing any corporate actions promptly and in accordance with its targets.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	prime brokerage services -&nbsp;Rehypothecation&nbsp;	re-charge, dispose of, or otherwise use the customer’s assets which are subject to the security, including disposing of them to a third-party.&nbsp;<br>When assets have been rehypothecated, they become the property of the prime broker as and when the prime broker uses them in this way, for instance, by depositing rehypothecated securities with a third-party financier to obtain cheaper funding&nbsp;<br><br>basically using a clients assets for themselves<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	systematic internaliser	investment firm (such as an investment bank) which, on an organised, frequent, systematic and substantial basis, deals on its own account when executing client orders instead of placing the orders elsewhere.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	why where systematic internalises intriduced	introduced by the EU Markets in Financial Instruments Directive (MiFID).&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	algorithmic trading	pre-built technical platforms to buy and sell shares automatically when predefined market conditions are met.&nbsp;<br><br>automated trading by computers which are programmed to take certain actions in response to varying market data.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	how does an algorithmic trading algo work	Market-predicting algorithms are created in order to find potential trades and then execute them. These algorithms are continually evolving through artificial intelligence and the sophisticated uses of statistics.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	why is algo trading popular	Removing the emotion and preserving the discipline of trading<br><br>Speed, accuracy and reduced costs	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	high frequency trading&nbsp;	use of powerful computers that are programmed to transmit orders based on algorithms.&nbsp;<br><br>These algorithms will respond extremely rapidly to market movements and, because the computers are usually located physically closely to those of the exchange, the orders will arrive ahead of other conventional orders.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	how does HFT work	high-frequency traders will enter hundreds, or even thousands, of small orders in this way and will then reverse the deals (so if the initial deals were to buy, they will sell or vice versa) to make a ‘turn’ on each.&nbsp;<br><br>amount of money made on each deal might be very small, but because of the quantity of deals being done, the high-frequency trader can make a lot of money very quickly.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	criticisms of HFT&nbsp;	seeing the traders as ‘vultures’ exploiting the genuine, longer-term investors and, given their dominance of market turnover, the impact they can have on pricing.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	HFT systemic risks	In the event of extreme price movements on one exchange, the arbitrage trades automatically placed by the high-frequency traders can rapidly spread the price movements to other exchanges where the same, or related, instruments are traded.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	latency&nbsp;	time taken to interact with the market. If latency can be lowered, it could give the trader the ability to act on market information more quickly than others and generate profits as a result.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	co location HFT	Co-location involves the trader using brokers with servers physically close to those of the exchange, often in the same data centre. This will minimise the data transmission time for orders to reach the exchanges servers.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	how do stock exchanges operate	electronic order-driven systems that automatically match orders to buy and sell equities.<br><br>electronic order book into which member firms submit their orders to buy and sell equities and, when there are orders that can be matched, the system automatically brings them together.<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	how are orders prioritised in an order book	orders are given priority first by price and then by time.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	how are opening prices established	auction process. Typically, this sees member firms entering orders in the period leading up to the auction. In this period, no trading takes place.<br><br>uses an uncrossing algorithm through which those orders that overlap on the order book are executed at the single price that maximises the number of shares traded.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	trading halt,&nbsp;	the exchanges reserved right&nbsp;to prohibit any transaction from being dealt on-exchange for any reason.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	Market orders	do not specify a price and are immediately executed at the best available price.&nbsp;<br><br>They are submitted to the order book to deal in a specified number of shares.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	(MOC).	market-on-close&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	(MOO)&nbsp;	market-on-open&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	Limit orders	price limit and a time limit, eg, a limit order may state: ‘sell 1,000 shares at 360 by next Tuesday’ and the system will attempt to sell these shares at a price no worse than 360 by next Tuesday.&nbsp;<br><br>Limit orders can be partially filled, and it is only limit orders that are displayed on the order book. Typically, and if no time limit is specified, limit orders are good only for the day on which they are ordered.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	what happens with limit orders with no time specified	limit orders are good only for the day on which they are ordered.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	Iceberg orders	limit order used by market participants with large order sizes to hide the size of their order from the market and reduce the market impact that the large order might otherwise have.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	how does an iceberg order work	the top part of the order is on view (the peak of the iceberg); the rest is hidden (the bulk of the iceberg is below the water).<br>Once the top part of the order is executed, the system automatically brings the next tranche of the iceberg order on to the order book.<br>This process continues until the whole of the iceberg order has been executed, or the time limit for the order expires.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	Execute and eliminate orders	– will execute as much of the trade as possible and cancel the rest.&nbsp;<br><br>unlike an at best order, this order type has a specified price and will not execute at a price worse than that specified.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	Fill-or-kill orders	a specified price,&nbsp;either the entire order will be immediately filled at a price at least as good as that specified, or the entire order will be cancelled&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	All-or-none orders	– require that an order is filled entirely, or not at all. All-or-none orders do not permit partial or multiple fills. If the entire order cannot be completed, it is not executed at all.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	‘novation’. example	A trade is executed on an exchange’s order book that involves A agreeing to sell shares to B. The CCP steps in between the two parties and two new obligations replace the initial obligation of A to sell to B. The two obligations are for A to sell to the CCP and then for the CCP to sell to B.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	benefits of a CCP	Reduced counterparty risk<br>Providing total anonymity<br>Reduced administration<br>Facilitating netting of transactions<br>Improved prices	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	CCP	Central counterparty	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	CCP Charges	charges a flat fee to both parties for fulfilling its role and also requires margin payments (similar to derivatives margin) to reduce its potential loss, should one party default.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	tacit fees&nbsp;	the difference between the bid and offer prices:&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	dealing costs	commission that needs to be paid to the broker arranging the transaction,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	Account fees&nbsp;	brokers also tend to charge account fees to their ongoing customers, although a number of brokers will waive these fees where the client undertakes more than a minimum number of trades during a particular period, such as a quarter or a year.&nbsp;<br><br>based on asset value or fixed cost	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	Broker’s commission	either based on a percentage of the value of the securities being purchased or sold or it is a flat fee.&nbsp;<br><br>Percentage-based commissions vary by broker, market, and often the size of order.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	full service brokers	brokers providing their clients with research and advice, higher commission	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	Stamp duties	tax that the buyer pays on certain trades, in some countries and jurisdictions.&nbsp;<br><br>usually embedded into the broker’s commission, collected by the financial institution, and payable to the government.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	stamp duty rate	For electronic share purchases in the UK, it is typically 0.5%,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	what investments have no stamp duty	ETFs	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	what exchanges charge stap duty	UK,Hong Kong and Swiss governments&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	what governments don't charge stamp duty	USA	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	(PIPs).&nbsp;	primary information providers&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	example of a PIP	LSE’s Regulatory New Service (RNS).	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	why are PIPs needed	Listed companies that have their shares traded on exchanges need to keep market participants posted on any price-sensitive information that may arise.&nbsp;<br><br>In the UK this is laid down in the FCA’s Disclosure Guidance and Transparency Rules sourcebook and in Europe it is a requirement of the European Union’s (EU’s) Transparency Directive.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	what do PIPs do for investors	offer a service that receives regulatory information from listed companies, processes that information and disseminates it by circulating it to secondary information providers (SIPs).&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	SIPs	secondary information providers&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	examples of SIPS	key vendors mentioned above: Bloomberg, Refinitiv and the Dow Jones. The SIPs, in turn, disseminate the information to the wider financial community, such as stockbrokers and research analysts.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	what is PIPs and SIPs info used for	used to inform and update research reports written by research analysts that comment on the likely future movements in the companies’ share prices.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	how long before an AIM company is removed for not having a NOMAD	1 month	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 4: PRIMARY MARKETS	municipalities bonds	guaranteed by a 3rd party - the US monoline insurer.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	how is bond dealing actioned	Direct telephone contact.<br><br>Indirect via an IDB voice-broking the deal.<br><br>Via an electronic market, known as an electronic trading platform, such as MTS Cash or BrokerTec.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	Dealer-to-customer trading&nbsp;	done either by voice trading between the two parties, or via an electronic platform, such as TradeWeb, MTS BondVision or proprietary single-dealer systems developed by some of the larger banks.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	financial information vendors&nbsp;	Bloomberg Fixed Income Trading (FIT), Liquidnet and MarketAxess.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	how is fixed income traded	fixed-income instruments had been undertaken over-the-counter (OTC) by dealers at large banks buying from, or selling to, clients that they had managed to establish relationships with.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	how are fixed income deals arranged	The deals were mostly arranged over the telephone, with clients often ringing more than one dealer in an attempt to find the most advantageous price.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	how would a dealer deal with other dealers	When dealers wanted to trade with other dealers, the typical method was to speak to a ‘voice broker’ at an inter-dealer broking firm, who would arrange deals with other dealers.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	how do&nbsp;dealer-to-customer systems operate	typically operate on a request for quote (RFQ) basis. Investors will request quotes from a number of dealers’ platforms simultaneously. Dealers respond to such requests very quickly and trades can be executed electronically.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	benefit of RFQ	RFQ systems also bring dealers into direct competition with each other which should deliver price improvement for investors.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	factors that effect credit pricing - issuer	Issuer’s current credit rating&nbsp;<br><br>The structure and seniority of the particular issue,&nbsp; (bonds may be high- or low-priority in the event of default by the issuer )<br><br>prevailing yields available on other benchmark bonds (gov bonds)<br><br>Macro economic and credit environment&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	market factors that effect credit pricing	Liquidity&nbsp;more liquid bonds tend to be more expensive,&nbsp;<br><br>Method of trading _ some bonds attract firm quotes while others are traded with indicative quotes only; the precise price will only be arrived at by negotiation.<br><br>Ability to borrow&nbsp;bonds with active repo markets, and the ability to cover short positions relatively easily, will inevitably react more quickly to underlying interest rate changes and therefore yield changes.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	stock market index	method of measuring the performance of a section of the stock market which is segmented to represent a particular group.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	how are indices weighted	Most are capitalisation-weighted, although some older indices are price-weighted.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	capitalisation weighted	‘Capitalisation-weighted’ refers to the importance of the constituents being based on their market capitalisation, which is the number of shares in issue multiplied by the price per share.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	price weighted	weights the constituents based on their per share prices.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	S&amp;P 500 index.&nbsp;	represents 500 American companies, each with a substantial market capitalisation, and described as leading stocks from the main industries.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	how can you be eligible for the s&amp;p500	meet market capitalisation requirements, be highly liquid, have a public float of at least 10% of its shares outstanding, and its most recent quarter’s earnings and the sum of its trailing four consecutive quarters’ earnings must be positive.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	market capitalisation requirement for S&amp;P 500 inclusion&nbsp;	$22.7 billion.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	how many stocks are in the s&amp;p500	504 stocks,&nbsp;since some companies have multiple share classes included.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	where are s&amp;p500 stocks traded	trade on the NYSE and the Nasdaq, and include most of the largest companies in the US.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	how is the s&amp;p500 weighted	weighted by free-float market capitalisation, so larger companies impact the index value more than smaller ones.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	Dow Jones Industrial Average (DJIA),&nbsp;	made up of only 30 companies that trade on US exchanges: either the NYSE or Nasdaq and is price weighted.&nbsp;<br><br>one of the oldest and most-referenced stock market indices in the world.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	FTSE Russell organisation&nbsp;	well known for maintaining several indices of US stocks, including the Russell 2000, which represents a diversified group of small capitalisation issues trading in US markets.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	MSCI	provider of global indices which is used heavily by institutional investors for benchmarking performance.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	Nikkei 225&nbsp;	premier index of Japanese stocks. It has been calculated for more than 60 years and consists of 225 stocks in the first section of the Tokyo Stock Exchange.&nbsp;<br><br>constituents are reviewed at the beginning of October each year, based on two factors: liquidity and sector balance.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	TOPIX&nbsp;	free float capitalisation-weighted index of all the companies on the Tokyo Stock Exchange’s large company board (first section).	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	FTSE 100 (Footsie) index,&nbsp;	consists of the 100 largest companies traded on the LSE as measured by market capitalisation.&nbsp;<br><br>represent more than 80% of the market capitalisation of the whole of the LSE.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	how is the FSTE 100 calculated	The index, maintained by FTSE Russell, is calculated in real time while the LSE is open for trading and published every 15 seconds. FTSE Russell is a part of the London Stock Exchange Group (LSEG).	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	FTSE valuation	FTSE 100 started at a base level of 1,000 points in January 1984, meaning that the value of the 100 constituent companies at that time equated to 1,000 index points.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	benefit of indices - prices	provide a quick and simple way of assessing whether the stock prices are moving up or down.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	hang seng index	Hong Kong/China<br><br>75 companies listed on the Hong Kong Stock Exchange selected on the basis of market value, turnover and financial performance	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	STOXX	Global developed markets<br><br>based around the STOXX Global 1800 index that consists of 600 of the largest capitalisation companies from each of three regions – Europe, the Americas and Asia/Pacific	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	FTSE Eurofirst 300	European-domiciled corporations<br><br>300 of the largest listed companies by market capitalisation from across Europe	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	(CAC) 40&nbsp;	French-domiciled companies; approximately 45% of its listed shares are owned by foreign investors, more than any other main European index	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	Wilshire 5000	Contains all US-headquartered equities with readily available prices	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	national index&nbsp;	performance of the stock market of a given nation and reflects investor sentiment on the state of its economy.<br><br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	example of a national index	Wilshire 5000 Index,&nbsp;<br><br>represents the stocks of nearly every publicly traded company in the US,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	specialised indices&nbsp;	track the performance of specific sectors of the market.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	examples of specialised indicies	FTSE EPRA/NAREIT for real estate and the Nasdaq Biotechnology Index for the biotechnology industry.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	construction of an index&nbsp;	involves summing the total market capitalisation of the companies with each company weighted by their individual value,&nbsp;<br><br>apart from DJIA - price weighted	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	how is the DJIA valued	sum of the prices of all 30 DJIA stocks is divided by the Dow Divisor.&nbsp;<br><br>sum of prices of component stocks / the dow divisor	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	criticisms of DJIA	being a price-weighted average, which gives higher-priced stocks more influence over the average than their lower-priced counterparts, but takes no account of the relative industry size or market capitalisation of the components.&nbsp;<br><br>For example, a $1 increase in a lower-priced stock can be negated by a $1 decrease in a much higher-priced stock, even though the lower-priced stock experienced a larger percentage change.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	total return index&nbsp;	calculates the performance of a group of stocks, assuming that dividends are reinvested into the index constituents.&nbsp;<br><br>For the purposes of index calculation, the value of the dividends is reinvested in the index on the ex-dividend date.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	Price return indices	measures the price performance and, therefore, disregards income from dividends.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	total return indicies	which measures the performance of both price return and dividend reinvestment.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	net total return	accounts for dividend reinvestment after the deduction of a withholding tax.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	free-float of a public company&nbsp;	an estimate of the proportion of shares that are not held by large owners and that are not burdened with sales restrictions&nbsp;<br><br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	how is a free float defined	all shares held by investors other than:<br>shares held by owners owning 5% or more of all shares (government holdings, institutional investors, strategic shareholders, founders)<br><br>restricted stocks&nbsp;<div><br>insider holdings (it is assumed that insiders hold stock for the very long term).<br><br></div>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	free-float factor&nbsp;	proportion of shares that is free-floated as a percentage of issued shares and is then typically rounded to the nearest multiple of 5%&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	example of free float factor	a constituent security with a free float of 23.2% will be included in the index at 25% of its total market capitalisation.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	how to find free float capitalisation of a company	first find its market cap (number of outstanding shares x share price) then multiply by its free-float factor.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	why is free float praised	provides a more accurate reflection of market movements and is more representative of the investable universe. When using a free-float methodology, the resulting market capitalisation is smaller than the full market capitalisation method. This is useful for performance measurement, as it provides a benchmark more closely related to what money managers can actually buy.
