CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	how are corporate bonds priced	price of a corporate bond is based on the equivalent government bond,<br>&nbsp;less a discount (eg, positive yield spread) to represent the risk that the corporate may default, compared with the default risk-free nature of the government bond.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	difference between equity and bond market	corporate bond usually has a specified income stream in the form of coupon payments which will be paid to the holder of the bond, and a bondholder has a more senior claim against the assets of the issuer in the case of a bankruptcy or restructuring.<br><br>equities may receive a dividend payment from the corporation, but this is less certain and can fluctuate. Indeed, less mature companies may not even pay a dividend. The equity-holder also has a greater risk&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	corporate bonds market makers	provide liquidity to the marketplace and to act as a facilitator or agent in trades between the principals.&nbsp;<br><br>Dealers are those that have been appointed by the corporate issuer to act as distributors on their behalf in the issuance and underwriting of bond issues.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	decentralised dealer market&nbsp;	enables investors to buy and sell without a centralised location. In a decentralised market, the technical infrastructure provides traders and investors with access to various bid/ask prices and allows them to deal directly with other traders/dealers rather than through a central exchange.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	example of a decentralised market,&nbsp;	FX -&nbsp;no single exchange or physical location where traders/investors have to conduct their buying and selling activities; trades can be conducted via an interbank/dealer network that is geographically distributed. Much of the trading in corporate bonds is also conducted through a decentralised dealer network that can provide pools of liquidity for the conduct of trade between buyers and sellers,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	LSE’s Order Book for Retail Bonds (ORB).	active retail market provided by stock exchanges for corporate bonds	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	participants in government bond markets	The government’s issuing agency<br>.Primary dealers – such as gilt-edged market makers (GEMMs) in the UK<br>.Broker-dealers.<br>Inter-dealer brokers.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	issuing agency for the UK government&nbsp;	The Debt Management Office (DMO)&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	The Debt Management Office (DMO)&nbsp;	executive agency of the UK Treasury, making new issues of UK government securities, which are known as ‘gilt-edged securities’ or just ‘gilts’.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	GEMM,&nbsp;	Gilt-Edged Market Makers (GEMMs),&nbsp;primary dealer and is required to provide two-way quotes to customers (clients known directly to it) and other member firms of the LSE throughout the normal trading day.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	obligations of a GEMM	make effective two-way prices to customers on demand, up to a size agreed with the DMO, thereby providing liquidity for customers wishing to trade.<br><br>participate actively in the DMO’s gilt issuance programme, broadly by bidding competitively in all auctions and achieving allocations&nbsp;<br><br>provide information to the DMO on closing prices, market conditions and the GEMM’s positions and turnover.<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	privileges of GEMM status&nbsp;	exclusive rights to competitively bid directly with the DMO at gilt auctions and other DMO operations,&nbsp;<br><br>exclusive facility to trade as a counterparty of the DMO in any of its secondary market operations,&nbsp;<br><br>exclusive access to the services of gilt inter-dealer brokers (IDBs).	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	Broker-Dealers	non-GEMM LSE member firms that are able to buy or sell gilts as principal (dealer) or as agent (broker).&nbsp;<br><br>broker-dealer must identify at the outset if the deal is a small one, defined as less than £1 million nominal.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	Gilt&nbsp; Inter-Dealer Brokers (IDBs)	arrange deals between gilt-edged market makers anonymously. They are not allowed to take principal positions, and the identity of the market makers using the service remains anonymous at all times.&nbsp;<br><br>will act as agent, but settle the transaction as if it were the principal.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	Government Issues in the European Union (EU)	European Commission issues bonds, referred to as EU bonds, to cover its funding needs.&nbsp;<br><br>EU bond issuances executed by the European Commission are denominated exclusively in euro.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	eurozone&nbsp;	Within the EU,&nbsp;consists of the 20 states which have adopted the euro as their currency and for whom their monetary policy is determined by monthly meetings of the European Central Bank (ECB).&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	German Government Securities	original maturities ranging from three months to 30 years.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	Bubills	German federal government issues Treasury discount paper&nbsp;with maturities of six and 12 months.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	German federal treasury notes	Schatz) with a maturity of two years, followed by five-year Federal notes (Bobls) (Bundesobligationen) and Federal bonds (Bunds) with maturities of ten and 30 years.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	how does the German government issue bonds	places single issues by auction. Only credit institutions domiciled in an EU member state can be members of the auction group and participate directly in these auctions.&nbsp;<br><br>The Bund uses a multiple price auction procedure. In other words, bids for Bunds, Bobls and Schatz accepted by the government are allocated at the price quoted in the respective bid and are not settled at a uniform price.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	how to&nbsp;remain a member of the auction group, german bonds	a credit institution must subscribe to at least 0.05% of the total issuance allotted at the auctions in a calendar year, weighted according to maturity.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	French Government Securities	Obligations Assimilable du Trésor (OATs) and Bons du Trésor à Taux Fixe et à Intérêts Précomptés(BFTs)	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	OATs	Obligations Assimilable du Trésor,&nbsp;government’s medium- and long-term debt instruments, with maturities from two to 50 years.&nbsp;<br><br>Most OATs are fixed-rate bonds redeemable on maturity. Longer-term OATs, with a maturity that exceeds eight years, are auctioned on the first Thursday of each month. Medium-term OATs and index-linked OATs are auctioned on the third Thursday of each month.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	The French Treasury&nbsp;	(Agency France Trésor)&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	BTFs,&nbsp;	Bons du Trésor à Taux Fixe et à Intérêts Précomptés<br><br>negotiable fixed-rate discount T-bills, are the government’s cash management instruments.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	Government Issues in the US	Treasury Department conducts auctions and direct sales of US Government securities.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	US government securities forms	bills, notes and bonds&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	Treasury bills (T-bills)&nbsp;	issued for terms less than a year.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	Treasury notes (T-notes)&nbsp;	issued for terms of two, three, five, seven and ten years.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	Treasury bonds (T-bonds)&nbsp;	issued for terms of 30 years.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	T-bills&nbsp;	issued in regular auctions with maturity dates of 28 days, 91 days, 182 days, 364 days<br><br>T-bills are sold by single price auctions held weekly.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	cash management bills&nbsp;	During periods when Treasury cash balances are particularly low,&nbsp;<br><br>sold at a discount and by auction just like weekly T-bills.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	T-notes and T-bonds interest	pay interest every six months until they mature. T-bonds have the longest maturity of 30 years. Both T-notes and T-bonds are issued by auction.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	Government Issues in Japan	Japanese Government bonds (JGBs) are issued by the Ministry of Finance (MoF)&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	types of&nbsp;JGBs&nbsp;	maturities of two, five, five (for retail investors), ten, ten (inflation-indexed), ten (for retail investors), 15 (floating rate), 20, 30 and 40 years.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 5: SECONDARY MARKETS	interest paid in JGBs	bonds issued by the government, which is responsible for interest and principal payments. Interest is paid every six months, and principal is repaid at maturity.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	income events	interest from bonds, dividends from stocks	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	FRNs&nbsp;	specify a coupon based on a published rate of interest, historically based on a margin above one of the London Interbank Offered Rates, LIBORs, or Eurozone equivalents, EURIBOR or more recently introduced reference rates like the Secured Overnight Financing Rate (SOFR).&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	bonds pay coupons&nbsp;	semi-annually or annually, and the coupon is a fixed percentage that is set when the bond is issued.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	income from equities&nbsp;	form of dividends is typically much less predictable than the income from bonds.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	Company dividends&nbsp;	variable in amount and will be dependent upon the profitability and cash flow generation of the issuer.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	benefits to the investors in equity&nbsp;	dividends tend to be predictable in terms of frequency and the amount can show some upside potential when the issuer does well.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	benefits to the issuer of equity&nbsp;	company can reduce the amounts, or even stop the payment of dividends, in times of lower profitability and/or the need to conserve cash.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	Preference shares&nbsp;	hybrid instrument because they are essentially a halfway house between equities and bonds. The income events for preference shares are dividend payments, but the rate of dividend will be specified in advance in a similar way to the coupons on a bond.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	capital event - bond	repayment of the principal borrowed.&nbsp;<br><br>usually occurs as a single lump-sum repayment of the entire principal at the maturity date specified in the bond’s contractual term.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	bullet payment.&nbsp;	single lump-sum repayment of the entire principal amount of a bond	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	non-bullet issues&nbsp;	repay the principal over a series of payments, rather than in a lump sum.&nbsp;<br><br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	riskiness - bullet versus non bullet	bullet issue bonds are considered to be riskier than non-bullet bonds.&nbsp;<br><br>Because bullet issues are riskier, they usually pay a higher rate of interest to compensate the lender.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	sinking fund&nbsp;	involves the issuer setting aside a certain amount of funds toward the maturity repayment each year.&nbsp;<br><br>The money is often paid to a separate trustee, who will either hold the money until the scheduled maturity date, or buy back bonds in the open market if they are trading below par.&nbsp;<br><br>sinking fund reduces the risk that the issuer will not have sufficient funds to repay the entire principal on the maturity date.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	callable bond&nbsp;	one where the issuer has the right, at specified points during the bond’s life, to redeem some, or all, of the bonds at a pre-agreed amount, often at par value.&nbsp;<br><br>Obviously, a call provision will give the issuer the ability to redeem a bond early if it is relatively expensive.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	putable bonds&nbsp;	give the bond investors the flexibility to require the bonds to be redeemed early, after giving the issuer due notice.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	types of equity capital events	All three – bonus issues, stock splits and reverse stock splits&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	bonus	bonus issue is where a company issues new shares to its shareholders for no consideration or pro bono, raising no further capital.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	references of bonus issue	bonus issue, a scrip issue or a capitalisation issue.&nbsp;<br><br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	reasons for bonus issue	sometimes it is as a public relations exercise to accompany news of a recent success or, more likely, it is simply as a means of reducing the current market price to make its shares more marketable.<br><br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	how does a&nbsp;bonus issue work	shareholders’ funds are shown in the company’s accounts, usually by converting undistributable capital reserves into share capital.&nbsp;<div><br></div><div>company simply converts its reserves, which may have arisen from issuing new shares in the past at a premium to their nominal value and/or from the accumulation of undistributed past profits, into new shares.&nbsp;</div>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	result of a bonus issue	nominal value of the company’s share capital will increase proportionately to the number of new shares issued,&nbsp;<div><br></div><div>given that the company’s earnings, or profits, and dividends will now be spread over a wider share capital base, the company’s earnings per share (EPS) and dividends per share (DPS) should fall proportionately with the number of new shares in issue.&nbsp;</div><div><br></div>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	when is a bonus issue used	when share price becomes too high - deemed too expensive for some investors - marketability	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	subdivision or stock split,&nbsp;	each share is split into a number of shares.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	why would a company do a stock split	share price has become too high, which impacts investor access and liquidity.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	example of a stock split	a company with shares having a nominal value of US$500 each and a market price of US$1,000 may have a split whereby each share is divided into five shares, each with a nominal value of US$100. In theory, the market price of each new share should be US$200 (US$1,000 ÷ 5). This is termed a ‘5-for-1 split’.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	effect of dividends on a stock split	Dividends paid on shares that split are adjusted proportionately to reflect the split.&nbsp;<div><br></div><div>Splits also impact options written on split shares&nbsp;</div>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	difference between stock split and bonus issue	bonus issue will increase the share capital line reflected in the accounts, with a corresponding movement from reserves. A stock split will not alter the share capital line – it will be the same total amount but will be subdivided into a larger number of shares, each with a smaller nominal value.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	cum bonus price&nbsp;	(also known as cum scrip or cum capitalisation). It is the price including the forthcoming bonus before the issue has happened.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	bonus issue simplified	moving cash reserves into company assets, to make more shares and reduce share price	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	example of bonus issue	before: shareholder has 3 shares, at £3 each - total value is £9<br><br>after a bonus issue of 1 share, shareholder now has 4 shares, but value is same so one share = £2.25, total value remains £9.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	how is a stock split calculated	dividing the existing share capital into a larger number of shares with a lower nominal value per share. The lower price should make the shares more accessible for investors&nbsp;<div><br></div><div><b>increase trading activity and liquidity for existing and prospective shareholders.</b></div>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	reverse stock split&nbsp;	consolidating the existing share capital into a smaller number of shares with a higher nominal value per share. The result should be a higher price per share.&nbsp;<div><br></div><div>Companies will frequently do this when the share price falls below a minimum price such as $1.00&nbsp;</div>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	pre-emptive rights.&nbsp;	legally required&nbsp;<br>give existing shareholders the right to subscribe for new shares.&nbsp;<br><br>means is that, unless the shareholders agree to permit the company to issue shares to others, they will be given the option to subscribe for any new share offering before it is offered to the wider public.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	purpose of pre emptive rights	ensure that the level of influence or control that a shareholder has is not diluted by any issue without that shareholder’s prior knowledge and agreement.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	what do pre emption rights stop	listed companies cannot issue equity shares, convertibles or warrants for cash, other than to the current equity shareholders of the company, except with the prior approval of the current shareholders&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	special resolution	proposal put to shareholders which requires 75% of the vote	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	rights issue&nbsp;	method by which a company can raise additional capital, complying with pre-emptive rights, with existing shareholders having the right to subscribe for new shares.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	what is a rights issue	offer by a company of new shares for cash to the existing shareholders in proportion to their existing holding.&nbsp;<br><br>shares are usually priced at a discount to the current market price. The holder of the right, as the name suggests, has the right, but not the obligation, to purchase additional shares directly from the company at the discounted price.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	why is a rights issue attractive	There is no dilution of shareholders’ interest,&nbsp;<br><br>The issue is at a discount to the current market&nbsp;<br><br>Existing ordinary shareholders of a company will receive a provisional allotment of new shares.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	rationale for rights issue	fund expansion, perhaps to take over a rival or to diversify into a new business area, or to raise more capital to enable the issuer to survive.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	how do shareholders qualify for a rights issue	only conferred upon those shareholders who hold the issuing company’s shares cum-rights – that is, those who hold the company’s shares before trading in the shares is conducted on an ex-rights, or without-rights, basis.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	when does cum rights period begin	begins on, or shortly after, the day on which the rights issue announcement is made and runs for a further period, tending to be a minimum of ten business days, through to the acceptance date,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	provisional allotment letter&nbsp;	entitled to participate in the rights issues are advised of their entitlement by a provisional allotment letter.&nbsp;<br><br>provisional allotment letter is renounceable and transferable and it sets out the shareholder’s existing shareholding, the rights allotted over the new shares and the acceptance date.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	what happens to share price - rights issue	once existing shares are declared ex rights, market price should fall to reflect dilution effect&nbsp;<br><br>price it falls to is called theoretical ex price	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	theoretical ex price calculation	[(No. shares held cum-rights x cum-rights share price) + (No. rights allocated x rights issue price)] / Total no. shares held assuming rights exercise	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	nil paid price	difference between the theoretical ex-rights price and the rights issue price&nbsp;<br><br>shareholders typically have a minimum of ten business days to decide how to react to the announcement following receipt of the provisional allotment letter&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	courses of action&nbsp;following receipt of the provisional allotment letter&nbsp;	Option One – Take up the rights in full<br>shareholder simply sends the company the provisional allotment letter, with a cheque, by the due date.<br><br>Option Two – Sell the rights nil-paid in full<br>sell the rights to these new shares nil-paid. The purchaser of the nil-paid rights will be able to take up the shares at the discounted price.&nbsp;<br><br>Option Three – Sell part of rights nil-paid to preserve current stake without dilution	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	formula for the theoretical ex-rights price&nbsp;	[(No. shares held cum-rights x cum-rights share price) + (No. rights allocated x rights issue price)] / Total no. shares held assuming rights exercise	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	nil-paid rights&nbsp;	theoretical value of the right to buy a share in a rights issue. This is calculated by comparing the theoretical ex-rights price to the price of exercising the right.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	(‘Swallowing the Tail’)	situation when investors can choose to sell some of their entitlement and use the cash raised to take up the rest of the offer. In effect, they can buy a sufficient number of shares in the offering to utilise the cash they receive from selling a proportion of their rights entitlement nil paid. They do not invest any additional funds into the business.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	share buyback	when a company decides to use cash to repurchase shares from existing shareholders.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	why would a company buy shares back	When the company has reduced its activities (perhaps having sold a major part of its business) and has surplus cash to return to shareholders.<br><br>When the company wants to reorganise its capital structure to include more debt and less equity.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	why are there restrictions on buybacks	to prevent shareholders from being unfairly preferred to creditors, and partly to make sure that the company has gained approval to buy back from its own shareholders.&nbsp;<br><br>regulation limits the monetary value that can be used to repurchase shares in a given year.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	ways a share buyback is constructed&nbsp;	Block trades<br>investment bank acting for the company will seek to do a small number of large trades with investors, perhaps through an exchange.<br><br>Accelerated book build<br>investment bank will contact a number of institutions that hold shares in the company, seeking their willingness to sell at particular price points. If the buyback is sufficiently large to require a syndicate (a group of dealers buying back shares, rather than just one), some of the more junior members may only be willing to be involved on a best-efforts basis, and the whole syndicate will have a price which it cannot go above (the back-stop price).&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	A stake&nbsp;	a shareholding, and many investors buy stakes in companies simply for the investment potential.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	Strategic stakes&nbsp;	accumulated in order to prevent a company being taken over by a competitor and to influence the company concerned.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	acquisition stake	stake is accumulated in the hope of bringing about an acquisition. An acquisition of another company is achieved by purchasing more than 50% of the shares, and thereby gaining control of the votes and the company.&nbsp;<br><br>A predator is unlikely to want the market to be aware of its intentions, as this might increase the price it has to pay to acquire shares in the target company.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	‘dawn raid’&nbsp;	purchasing a large number of shares as soon as the market opens, or at least over a short period of time before their intentions become clear.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	build a stake in an ‘indirect’&nbsp;	stake could be established by acquiring contracts for difference&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	CFD&nbsp;	an agreement between the buyer and seller to exchange the difference in the current value of the share and its value at the end of the contract.&nbsp;<br><br>If the difference is positive, the seller pays the buyer; if it is negative, the buyer pays the seller.&nbsp;<br><br>CFDs enable participants to get the economic exposure of owning a stake in another entity in a less visible and potentially leveraged way.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	‘M&amp;A’&nbsp;	a merger and/or acquisition or any blending of two existing companies.&nbsp;<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS			
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	takeover/aquisition	occurs if one company buys a majority of the shares of another company; it gains control over the other company and is, therefore, termed the parent company,&nbsp;<br><br>could be ‘friendly’ because the directors of the target company are positive about the merits of the takeover and recommend their shareholders accept it. Alternatively, it could be ‘hostile’ because the directors of the target company recommend rejecting the offer,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	merger	two companies of a similar size come together to form a larger, merged entity.&nbsp;<br><br>it is usual for one company to exchange new shares for the shares of the other. As a result, the two companies effectively come together to form a bigger entity under the joint ownership of the two original groups of shareholders.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	key benefits of creating a business combination by acquisition or merger&nbsp;	Increasing market share<br><br>Cost savings<br><br>Expansion into new products and markets	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	disadvantages of combining companies in a merger or acquisition&nbsp;	Overpaying<br><br>Cultural clashes<br><br>Diseconomies of scale<br>little in common between the companies, it may be difficult to find economies of scale and the combined entity might be geographically dispersed and add to, rather than reduce, the cost base.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	monopoly&nbsp;	occurs when one entity controls the whole of the market for a particular good or service.&nbsp;<br><br>Since consumers have no competitors, the entity can charge whatever price it wants and the customers have no choice but to pay.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	how are people protected from monopolies	regulations that protect consumers from monopoly-type activity are referred to as ‘antitrust’ laws in the US and enforced by an agency called the Federal Trade Commission. In the UK, the Competition and Markets Authority (CMA) is the regulatory body that fulfils a similar role.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	antitrust laws - US	prevent the creation of anti competitive entities	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	federal trade comission	antitrust regulator in the USA	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CAHPTER 6: CORPORATE ACTIONS	competition and markets authority (CMA)	antitrust regulator in UK	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	how does settlement occur	after a deal has been executed.&nbsp;<br><br>change in economic ownership is immediate, the transfer of securities from the seller and the payment from the buyer takes some time.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	Pre-settlement and clearing	soon as a trade has been executed, a number of procedures and checks must be conducted before settlement can be completed.&nbsp;<br><br>matching the trade instructions supplied by each counterparty to ensure that the details they have supplied for the trade correspond. It also involves conducting checks to ensure that the seller has sufficient securities to deliver and that the buyer has sufficient funds to cover the purchase cost.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	Settlement&nbsp;	legal title (ie, ownership) of a security is transferred from seller to buyer in exchange for the equivalent value in cash.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	Post-settlement	management of failed transactions and the subsequent accounting of trades.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	‘trade confirmation’ step.	two sides to the trade to compare trade details, and to eliminate any mismatches prior to the exchange of cash and securities.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	how do&nbsp;Trading counterparties compare trade details&nbsp;	matching facilities extended by the securities settlement system or a&nbsp;third-party central matching facility that will compare trade details electronically and issue a report on matching status&nbsp;<br><br>Trades conducted via an electronic order book will effectively be auto-matched&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	how do custodians compare details	Custodians acting on behalf of the buyer and seller will compare settlement instructions in order to identify potential mismatches prior to the settlement date.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	clearing	process through which the obligations held by the buyer and seller to a trade are defined and legally formalised.&nbsp;<br>establishes what each of the counterparties expects to receive when the trade is settled.&nbsp;<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	risk with bilateral trade clearing	Trades may be cleared bilaterally between the trading counterparties or via a CCP that interposes itself between buyer and seller. When trades are cleared bilaterally, each trading party bears a direct credit risk against each counterparty that it trades with.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	CCP services&nbsp;	central counterparty, novation of trades and matching&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	CCP service providers - US	Depository Trust and Clearing Corporation (DTCC)&nbsp;<br><br>National Securities Clearing Corporation (NSCC) provides these services for US equities trades&nbsp;<br><br>Fixed Income Clearing Corporation (FICC) for US fixed income transactions.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	CCPs providers UK	(LCH ltd),&nbsp;<br><br>EquityClear that can be used for shares traded on both the LSE and the Euronext European markets.&nbsp;<br><br>SwapClear for OTC interest rate swaps, and CDSClear for credit default swaps (CDSs).	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	Eurex Clearing AG,&nbsp;	part of Deutsche Börse Group,&nbsp;deals with German equities and derivatives.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	CME Group&nbsp;	in the US clears futures and options trades on the CME group exchanges, but also provides clearing services for certain OTC trades such as interest rate swaps and foreign exchange transactions.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	how can Timing of settlement differ	length of time it takes for a trade to settle is based on the trade date plus a set number of business days after the trade is executed.&nbsp;<br><br>T+2 means transaction date plus two business days. Settlement timing differs across different countries/exchanges&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	how can the structure of the settlement system effect settlement	structural approaches that are typically used for linking delivery and payment&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	(DvP).&nbsp;	delivery versus payment, assets for cash	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	Delivery versus payment (DvP)&nbsp;	making delivery (transferring ownership) when the payment is certain, and paying over the cash when the transfer of ownership is assured.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	DVP settlement system use	buyer of shares and the seller of those shares, both face risks. The seller faces the risk that the buyer may not pay for the shares and the buyer faces the risk that the seller may not pass over ownership of the shares.<br><br>system that guarantees DvP will remove those risks by only transferring ownership of the shares when the payment is certain, and only making the payment when the transfer of ownership is certain.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	models for DVP systems	Model 1 – systems that settle transfer instructions for both securities and funds on a trade-by-trade (gross) basis, with final (unconditional) transfer of securities from the seller to the buyer (delivery) occurring at the same time as final transfer of funds from the buyer to the seller (payment).&nbsp;<br><br>Model 2 – systems that settle securities transfer instructions on a gross basis, with final transfer of securities from the seller to the buyer (delivery) occurring throughout the processing cycle, but settle funds transfer instructions on a net basis, with final transfer of funds from the buyer to the seller (payment) occurring at the end of the processing cycle.&nbsp;<br><br>Model 3 – systems that settle transfer instructions for both securities and funds on a net basis, with final transfers of both securities and funds occurring at the end of the processing cycle.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	how does CREST use DVP example	CREST is a computer system that settles transactions in shares, gilts and corporate bonds for LSE<div><br></div><div>CREST simultaneously:<br><br></div><div><div><br></div><div>•&nbsp; &nbsp; Updates the register of shareholders – CREST maintains the so-called ‘operator register’ for UK companies’ dematerialised shareholdings</div><div>•&nbsp; &nbsp; Issues a payment obligation – CREST sends an instruction to the buyer’s payment bank to pay for the shares<br>issues a notification to sellers bank to expect payment</div></div>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	shorter settlement for equities	May 2024, the US and Canada moved from a T+2 settlement period to T+1 for equities&nbsp;<br><br>This change is in response to expanded technology, and an effort to reduce credit risk, market risk and liquidity risks associated with unsettled trades.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT			
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	custodian role	responsibility of the custodian is to keep its clients' assets safe, ensuring that they are fully protected at all times.&nbsp;<br><br>it must provide robust safekeeping facilities for all of the client’s physical and digital assets, ensuring that investments are only released from its care in accordance with authorised instructions from the client.<br><br>each client’s assets must be properly segregated from those of the custodian and appropriate legal arrangements must be in place to ensure that financial or external shock to the custodian does not expose the client’s assets to claims from creditors or any other party.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	custodians services	providing safekeeping of the investor’s assets in the local market<br><br>lists of inventory<br><br>verifying trade details&nbsp;<br><br>ensuring that securities are registered and that transfer of legal title on securities transactions proceeds effectively<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	local custodian&nbsp;<br><br>	custodian in market they are trading in, i.e. US custodian for US assets<br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	global custodian&nbsp;	the full range of foreign markets in which they have invested assets.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	CSD&nbsp;	Making arrangements to settle trades and hold securities and cash with a CSD within each market,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	global custodian&nbsp;	processing cross-border securities trades and keeping financial assets secure outside the country where the investor is located.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	largest firms that operate as global custodians&nbsp;	JP Morgan Chase, Citi, BNY Mellon and State Street.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	term ‘global custody’&nbsp;	came into common usage in the financial services world in the mid-1970s, when the Employee Retirement Income Security Act (ERISA) was passed in the US.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	Employee Retirement Income Security Act (ERISA)&nbsp;	This legislation was designed to increase the protection given to US pension fund investors. The Act specified that US pension funds could not act as custodians of the assets held in their own funds. Instead, these assets had to be held in the safekeeping of another bank.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	sub-custodian&nbsp;	employed by a global custodian as its local agent to provide settlement and custody services for assets that it holds on behalf of investor clients in a foreign market.&nbsp;<br><br>serves as the eyes and ears of the global custodian in the local market,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	Local Custodian/single market providers	Agent banks that specialise in providing sub-custody in their home market&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	Local Custodian examples	MUFG Bank, Mizuho Bank and Sumitomo Mitsui Banking Corporation in Japan, Maybank in Malaysia and United Overseas Bank in Singapore.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	principal selling point local custodians	local market specialists. Hence, they can remain focused on their local business, without spreading their attention broadly across a wide range of markets.&nbsp;<br><br>local specialist bank may be attractive in a market in which local practices tend to differ markedly from global standards, or where a provider’s long-standing relationship with the local regulatory authorities and/or political elite leaves it particularly well placed to lobby for reforms on behalf of its cross-border clients.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	regional custodian&nbsp;	provide agent bank services across multiple markets in a region.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	example of regional custodian	Standard Chartered Bank and HSBC have both been offering regional custody and clearing in the Asia-Pacific and South Asian region for many years, competing with Citi and some strong single market providers for business in this region.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	company registrar.&nbsp;	issuing company maintains a register listing all of its shareholders and their legal addresses.&nbsp;<br><br>Whenever shares are bought or sold, the company registrar is responsible for updating the register of members and addresses and giving the new owner registered title.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	bearer shares,&nbsp;	Shares that are not registered&nbsp;<br>the person holding the shares is the owner and there are no records to confirm or contradict this.&nbsp;<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT			
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	Bearer shares&nbsp;	like cash, in that if they are given away, lost or stolen, the person who physically has them in hand is able to benefit from their monetary value and any associated voting rights.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	issues with bearer shares	attraction of bearer shares to those that might want to keep ownership secret, such as those that might want to hide their ill-gotten gains,&nbsp;<br><br>banned in some markets, like UK	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	Registered title,&nbsp;	ownership that is backed by registration, whereas bearer form means in possession of the physical share certificates without any underlying register entry.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	nominee&nbsp;	takes the registered title to the shares and all the responsibilities that go with it, but the nominee’s client retains beneficial ownership. The beneficial owner receives dividends and is entitled to any other ‘benefits’ of the shares such as the ability to vote or participate in other corporate actions. The nominee is, however, the legal owner.&nbsp;<br><br>The legal owner has the title of the shares but as nominee, has no rights to any of the benefits of owning the shares.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	nominee shareholders&nbsp;	will hold the shares in trust for the beneficial owners, and it is the nominee shareholders that are identified on the register of shareholders. however the investor is the beneficiary of being a shareholder	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	declaration of trust&nbsp;	signed by nominee shareholder, to beneficial owner&nbsp;<br><br>declaration of trust to the beneficial owner that they are holding the shares on behalf of the beneficial owner and will return the shares into the name of the beneficial owner or will transfer them to another party as requested.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	example of nominees	Institutional investors employing professional investment management firms to manage their assets are highly unlikely to hold these securities in their own name (name on register).&nbsp;<br><br>the person whose name appears on the share register receives every piece of documentation sent out by the company and is obliged to sign all share transfers and other relevant forms such as instructions for rights issues and other corporate events. To ensure safe custody of assets and remove this administrative burden from the investor, thus allowing the speedy processing of transfers - held by nominee companies - investment managers	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	Nominee companies&nbsp;	how&nbsp;asset managers and custodians can process transactions on behalf of their clients. Given that many investment management firms have outsourced some or all of their investment administration activities to the specialist custodians, the vast majority of institutional shareholdings in fact now reside in nominee accounts&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	pooled nominee company	individual clients are grouped together within a single nominee registration.<br><br>(often termed ‘omnibus’ or ‘commingled’),&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	Designated&nbsp; nominee	nominee name includes unique identifiers for each individual client, eg, XYZ Nominees Account 1, Account 2, Account 3.<br><br>(sometimes termed ‘numbered accounts’),&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	Sole nominee	single nominee name is used for a specific client, eg, ABC Pension Fund Nominees ltd.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	benefit of using a sole nominee name	if the underlying investor requires dividends to be mandated to a particular bank account rather than being collected by the custodian, registration in an omnibus account is not practicable.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	pooled nominee arrangements advantage	Operating a designated nominee account should give rise to few additional costs from the custodian’s point of view, from repeated admin work. cheaper to pool together	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	corporate nominee&nbsp;	(alternatively referred to as a corporate sponsored nominee)&nbsp;<br><br>issuing company itself provides a facility for its smaller shareholders to hold their shares within a single corporate nominee.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	what does a corporate nominee do	single entry for all the shareholders together in the company’s register (like the pooled nominee) but beneath this the issuing company (or its registrar) will be aware of the individual holdings that make up the nominee.&nbsp;<br><br>company makes the polled account, not the custodian/asset manager	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	dematerialised form	securities are held electronically without certificates	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	Custodians and their nominees control	control the majority of share registrations for institutional investors, even for clients who may not have directly appointed custodians but whose asset management firms have outsourced their investment administration to these providers.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	how do&nbsp;Custodians identify their clients’ holdings&nbsp;	segregating these in their computer systems, as it is largely these systems which drive the calculation and application of dividends and other entitlements.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	single nominee registration	omnibus account.&nbsp;<br><br>custodians prefer to pool all their clients into one account and registar	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	Cum-dividend&nbsp;	cumulative dividend, with the dividend.<br><br>Shares are normally traded cum-dividend, meaning that buyers of the shares will receive the next dividend paid by the company.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	ex-dividend,&nbsp;	that the buyer would not receive the next dividend. The ex-dividend period occurs just prior to a dividend payment.<br><br>u have to hold an asset before ex date to be entitled to the div	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	disadvantage of special cum trades and special ex trades&nbsp;	they will, potentially, result in dividends from the company being paid to the wrong person. Equally a trade that settles later than usual could mean that the correct owner is not reflected in the shareholders’ register on the books- closed date – and the dividend is paid by the company to the wrong person.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	cum-trade.&nbsp;	the buyer of the share during the ex-dividend period does receive the next dividend. by special arrangement<br><br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	special ex trade.&nbsp;	only possible in the ten business days before the ex-date. If an investor buys a share during the cum-dividend period, but buys it special ex, they will not receive the next dividend.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	special cum or special ex transactions uses	enables the sellers or buyers to avoid the receipt of a dividend – essentially deciding whether or not they want to collect their right to the dividend. tax incentives&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	volume in the global FX market, and related markets,&nbsp;	approximately US$9.6 trillion per day in 2025, an increase of 28% since the previous survey in 2022,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	continuous linked settlement	process by which most of the world’s largest banks manage FX settlement among themselves (and their customers and other third parties).&nbsp;<br><br>process is managed by CLS Group Holdings and regulated by the Federal Reserve Board of New York.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	how are CLS settled	payment versus payment (PvP) basis.&nbsp;<br><br>two parties to an FX transaction will buy and sell the respective currencies exchanged and the payments made will occur simultaneously.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	Herstatt risk.&nbsp;	the settlement risk if PvP settlement payments are not simultaneous	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	what does CLS ensure	payment from one party was only made if there was a payment coming in the opposite direction to fulfil the other side of the FX deal&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	CLS process timeframe	focused on a five-hour window each business day from 7.00am to 12 midday in Central European Time (CET). This window was created to provide an overlap across the business days in all parts of the world and facilitate global trading.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	Distributed ledger technology&nbsp;	"replacement of one, centralised ledger of transactions with a decentralised network of computers all holding copies of exactly the same ledger.&nbsp;<br><br>computers are called ""nodes"" in this scenario"	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	example of a distributed ledger	Blockchain is an example of DLT and is commonly associated with the cryptocurrency Bitcoin. Bitcoin is a public system that uses blockchain DLT and a consensus algorithm based on proof of work.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	advantages of distributed ledger technology DLT	Produce a trustworthy and reliable record, since consensus is required for any changes.<br><br>Be very difficult to hack, because of the use of multiple nodes.<br><br>Remove the costs and delays caused by the need to maintain and update a single, central database.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	DLT future advancements	DLT could be adopted to settle trades and may be able to speed up settlement and reduce settlement costs. Original versions of DLT had an ever-expanding blockchain so verifying transactions took an increasing amount of time. However, other types of consensus algorithms have since been introduced which speed up the process.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	Stock lending&nbsp;	temporary transfer of securities,&nbsp;<br><br>commonly seen in the more developed markets where liquidity is greatest and regulations easily allow for shorting.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	main motivations for stock lending	securities-driven<br><br>cash-driven	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	securities driven transactions	borrowing firms seek specific securities&nbsp;to facilitate their trading operations. (short)<br><br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	cash-driven transactions	lender is able to increase the returns on an underlying portfolio, by receiving a fee for lending out the goods	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	securities lending agreement,&nbsp;	sets out&nbsp;The terms of the securities loan&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	features of a securities lending agreement	requires that the borrower provide the lender with collateral, in the form of cash, government securities, or a letter of credit of value equal to or greater than the loaned securities.&nbsp;<br><br>parties negotiate a fee, quoted as an annualised percentage of the value of the loaned securities.&nbsp;<br>If the agreed form of collateral is cash, then the fee may be quoted as a rebate,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	Benefits of Stock Lending	initial driver for the securities lending business was to cover settlement failure.&nbsp;<br><br>To avoid the costs and penalties associated with settlement failure, stock can be borrowed for a fee in order to meet a delivery commitment.&nbsp;<br><br>Once the original stock finally arrives (or is obtained from another source), the borrower can return the same number of shares to the lender that were originally borrowed.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	short position	cover a sale where a seller does not have the securities sold&nbsp;<br><br>sellers taking short positions are obliged to deliver the securities they do not hold, they will have to borrow them.&nbsp;<br><br>selling securities that are not held at the time of sale.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	disclosures of short positions	In Europe, disclosures to the regulator start at net short positions amounting to 0.1% of the issued share capital of a listed company. UK disclosures do not start until they reach 0.2% of the issued share capital of a listed company. In both the UK and Europe, net short positions are publicly disclosed when they reach 0.5% of the issued share capital.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	positive aspects of stock lending:	can increase the liquidity of the securities market&nbsp;<br><br>can provide extra security to lenders through the collateralisation of a loan.<br><br>can support many trading and investment strategies&nbsp;<br><br>allows investors to earn income by lending their securities&nbsp;<br><br>facilitates the hedging and arbitraging of price differentials,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	associated risks with stock lending	collateral, may not be returned on the agreed date, whether because of settlement delays or liquidity problems in the market&nbsp;<br><br>or counterparty default or legal challenge. <br><br>securities will then need to be acquired in the market, potentially at a high cost.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	stock lending risk&nbsp;mitigations	employing detailed credit evaluations on the borrower<br><br>setting limits on the lender’s credit exposure&nbsp;<br><br>collateralising loan exposures against cash or securities&nbsp;<br><br>marking exposures and collateral to market&nbsp;<br><br>employing master legal agreements that set out clear legal parameters that dictate the structure and process of the lending arrangements,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	‘haircut’.&nbsp;	protects the lender from adverse price movements of the collateral held.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	stock borrowing and lending intermediaries&nbsp;(SBLIs)&nbsp;	provide a service in separating out the underlying owners of securities&nbsp;from those who would be borrowers of those securities, typically hedge funds and other asset managers, and liaising with both sides.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	benefits of&nbsp;stock borrowing and lending intermediaries&nbsp;	economy of scale offered by SBLIs in pooling together securities of different clients has also enabled smaller asset holders to participate in this market.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	advantages of acting as an SBLI for custodians	SBLI can add a new level of revenue generation for custodian banks.&nbsp;<br><br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	SBLI obligations	If assets are pooled within an SBLI, the SBLI must ensure that only assets belonging to customers who have consented to securities lending are lent out.&nbsp;<br><br>SBLI should maintain a separate account or be able to demonstrate that it maintains adequate systems to differentiate between the safe custody investments of those customers who have not consented to stock lending activity&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	permitted purposes of stock lending	facilitate settlement of a trade<br><br>facilitate delivery of a short sale<br><br>finance the security<br><br>facilitate a loan to another borrower&nbsp;<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	corporate actions when borrowing shares	title of the security transfers to the borrower.&nbsp;<br><br>borrower has the advantages of holding the security, just as though they owned it -&nbsp;borrower will receive all coupon and/or dividend payments, and any other rights such as voting rights.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	securities lending, lender vs borrower	lender effectively retains all the benefits of ownership. The borrower can use the securities as required – perhaps by lending them on to another party – but is liable to the lender for all the benefits such as dividends, interest and stock splits.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	Global Master Securities Lending Agreement (GMSLA)&nbsp;	developed as a market standard for securities lending. It covers the matters which a legal agreement ought to cover for securities lending transactions.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	sale/repurchase agreements&nbsp;	(repos)&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	Stock Lending Versus Repo	key difference is that a stock lender charges a fee to the borrower, whereas a repo counterparty pays (or receives) a rate of interest.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	repurchase agreement,&nbsp;	method of secured lending,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT			
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	how does a repo transaction work	securities are sold to a counterparty against payment of cash and then repurchased at a later date, with interest being paid to the cash lender.&nbsp;<br><br>swap cash and assets, asset lender gains interest on cash	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	cash borrower - repo transaction	provides a method of funding that is significantly cheaper than borrowing on an unsecured basis. The cash lender receives securities as collateral throughout the loan period and is free to utilise these securities if the cash borrower fails to repay cash plus interest&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	collateral’s value in repo	(marked-to-market) on a daily basis throughout the repo period to reflect current market values. If the value of the collateral rises or falls outside of a pre-agreed band (known as the variation margin), a margin call will be made&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	variation margin	pre-agreed band value of the assets used in a repo	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 7: CLEARING AND SETTLEMENT	margin call&nbsp;	collateral is requested from, or returned to, the lender&nbsp;to ensure that the value of the collateral remains aligned to the cash sum borrowed through the repo agreement.
