CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	accounting definition&nbsp;	recording, measuring and reporting of economic events, or activities, to interested parties in a usable form.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	purpose of accounting	providing information relating to the financial and economic activities of a business in the form of a set of accounts.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	who should prepare financial statements	directors of a company are required to prepare and maintain financial statements and make other disclosures through periodic reports, including an annual report.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	3 major statements	The statement of financial position/balance sheet<br><br>The income statement<br><br>A cash flow statement	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	The statement of financial position/balance sheet	snapshot of the company’s financial position as of a given date, usually the company’s accounting year-end.<br><div><br></div><div>made up of assets, equity and liabilities<br><br>assets owned by company,&nbsp;<br><br>equity is the total capital amount that the shareholders have contributed and are due.&nbsp;<br><br>liabilities - debt</div>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	The income statement	summarises incomings and outgoings.&nbsp;<br><br>summary of the trading activities of the company over the year. If income exceeds expenses, the company has made a profit; if expenses exceed income, the company has incurred a loss.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	cash flow statement	identifies how much cash the company generated over the accounting period and how much cash has been spent.&nbsp;<br><br>cash flow from operating, investing and financing activities.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	requirements of financial statements	required to be independently verified, or audited.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	audit	independent assessment of the company’s accounts that have been prepared by the directors.&nbsp;<br><br>confirming whether or not the accounts give a true and fair view of the company’s activities and financial position and whether they have been prepared in accordance with the law and other regulations.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	fail/passing an audit	if passed,&nbsp;unqualified audit report is issued. If they have not the auditor must issue a qualified report and state the reason for this qualification.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Assets&nbsp;	items owned or controlled by the company. Technically they are often defined as items that are expected to yield economic benefit in future periods.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Liabilities&nbsp;	money owed by the company to others.&nbsp;<br><br>debt/taxes	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Equity&nbsp;	amount invested in a business by its owners, plus any earnings made by the company that have not been paid out as dividends.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Revenue&nbsp;	sales made by a company. It represents an increase in assets or decrease in liabilities brought about by providing services or selling products to customers.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Expenses&nbsp;	costs of doing business. Expenses include cost of goods sold, rent on property, wages and salaries, and interest on borrowing.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Accounting standards&nbsp;	authoritative statements of how transactions should be reported in financial statements	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	(GAAP).&nbsp;	generally accepted accounting principles&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	IFRS Foundation,&nbsp;	non profit established to help create a globalised account and sustainability standard	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	The ISSB&nbsp;	standard-setting board, reporting by companies on ESG metrics	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	The IASB&nbsp;	developing a single set of&nbsp;enforceable global accounting standards&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	IASB members&nbsp;	chair and an internationally diverse set of members with a variety of functional backgrounds.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	US GAAP	American reporting standard, differs slightly to IFRSs	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	group accounts&nbsp;	reporting needed for when a company has a parent company<br><br>investing company is described as the parent and the company or companies that the parent company controls are described as subsidiaries. As long as a parent/subsidiary relationship exists, the parent company should prepare and present a set of group accounts in addition to its individual company financial statements.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	benefit of group accounts	present the financial statements as if the parent and the subsidiaries were a single entity,&nbsp;<br><br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Goodwill&nbsp;	intangible asset that represents the value of the company’s brand,&nbsp;<br><br>recorded on a balance sheet when one company is buying another.&nbsp;<br><br>if cost to buy out company is bigger than companies net assets - surplus is put as goodwill on financial statement.<br><br>e.g. google buy meta for 300m, metas net assets = 200m, then goodwill would be 100m on their group account	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Non-controlling interests	when parent company owns majority stake, non controlling are the shares they dont own. e.g. google owns 52% of meta, Meta non controling interests are 48%.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	balance sheet,&nbsp;	snapshot of a company’s financial position at a particular moment, usually a company’s quarter end or year end.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	balance sheet sections	divided in two, and the two sections must always balance each other exactly, hence the term ‘balance sheet’.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	owns vs owes	assets vs liabilities	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	current vs non current	assets and liabilities are also divided into current (due within one year) and non-current, to provide additional context as to how liquid or permanent the assets are, and how immediately the liabilities are due.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	asset&nbsp;	anything that is owned and controlled by the company&nbsp;<br><br>Statement of financial position assets are categorised as either non-current assets or current assets.<br><br>e.g. land,raw materials, machinery etc	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Non-current assets&nbsp;	long-term, continuing use by the company.&nbsp;<br><br>major investments from which the company hopes to earn money,&nbsp;<br><br>can be categorised as tangible/non tangible	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Tangible Non-Current Assets	physical substance, such as land and buildings and plant and machinery, and indeed are often referred to as plant, property and equipment (PPE).&nbsp;<br><br>recorded in the statement of financial position at their actual cost, or book value.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Intangible non-current assets	assets that are expected to generate economic benefits over a number of accounting periods but are without physical substance.&nbsp;<br><br>examples include computer software, patents, trademarks, capitalised development costs and purchased goodwill.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Purchased goodwill&nbsp;	paid by an acquiring company for a target subsidiary company, exceeds the fair value of the target’s separable, or individually identifiable, net assets.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Non-current investments&nbsp;	investible assets, such as equity investments or investments in debt instruments in other companies, that will not be sold and will not mature within the next year.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Current assets&nbsp;	purchased with the intention of resale or conversion into cash, usually within a 12-month period.&nbsp;<br><br>such as finished goods ready to sell,&nbsp;cash balances held by the company and prepayments.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Prepayments&nbsp;	when the company has prepaid an expense,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	how are current assets listed in statement	ascending order of liquidity and appear in the statement of financial position at the lower of cost or net realisable value (NRV).	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	depreciation	fall in value of a non current asset like a machine, use over time will erode value, value is adjusted and incurs a cost on the income statement over time	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	amortisation	depreciation against non - current intangible assets	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Depreciation and amortisation costs&nbsp;	recorded as expenses on each year’s income statement, because using up the value of the asset, like equipment, is considered a cost of performing business, just as wages being paid to employees are.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Straight line depreciation equation	( cost – disposal value ) / useful economic life in years	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	revaluation reserve,&nbsp;	when&nbsp;tangible assets, like land, go up in price - increase is put under revaluation reserve, under equity - so equity, assets and liabilities all balance	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	intangible asset Amortisation,&nbsp;	book entry whose impact is felt in the company’s reported income and financial position but does not impact its cash position.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	intangible assets examples	software, patents and licences held by the company.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	equity examples	shareholders’ funds, owners’ equity or capital.&nbsp;<br><br>three sub-elements: share capital, capital reserves and revenue reserves.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Share capital	nominal value of equity and preference share capital the company has in issue and has called up.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Capital reserves	revaluation reserves, and share premium account t (money made if shares issued higher than fair value). money is kept separate in case needed for bonus issue.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Revenue reserves	retained earnings from company -&nbsp;accumulation of the company’s distributable profits that have not been paid to the company’s shareholders as dividends, but have been retained in the business.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Non-controlling interests	these arise when a parent company controls one or more subsidiary companies, but does not own all of the share capital.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	equity equation	Equity = share capital + reserves.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Non-current liabilities	company’s borrowing not repayable within the next 12 months.&nbsp;<br><br>e.g. bond issuance/long term loans	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Current liabilities	amount the company owes to its suppliers, or trade payables, as a result of buying goods and/or services on credit, any bank overdraft, and any other payables, such as tax, that fall due for payment within 12 months of the date of the statement of financial position.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	provisions.&nbsp;	old liabilities that still need payment<br><br>may arise as a result of the company undergoing a restructuring, for example. can be adjusted	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	income statement&nbsp;	summarises companies revenues and expenses<br><br>referred to as the profit and loss statement,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	function of financial statement	detail the company’s profits and losses.&nbsp;<br><br>shows earnings per share which is what shareholders will often look to first, and is where the reference to ‘the bottom line’ comes from.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	what does profit determine	health of the company,&nbsp;how well its people and policies are performing, dividends,&nbsp;how much it can retain to finance the growth&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	revenue	everything that the company has sold during the year, regardless of whether it has received the cash or not. sale of product/services	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	costs of sales&nbsp;	costs to the company of generating the sales made in the financial year.&nbsp;<br><br>raw materials, wages,&nbsp;does not include indirect expenses such as the costs of distribution or adminstrative or financial expenses.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	gross profit	Total sales, less the costs of those sales, results in the gross profit for the year.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Operating profit&nbsp;	profit on operating activities.&nbsp;<br><br>gross profit - operating expenses like distribution costs, management salaries, auditors fees etc<br><br>Administrative expenses would also include depreciation and amortisation charges.&nbsp;<br><br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	(PBIT)&nbsp;	profit before interest/tax<br><br>Operating profit is the profit before considering finance costs (interest) and any tax payable – so it can also be described as profit before interest and tax (PBIT) or earnings before interest and tax (EBIT).&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	(EBITDA).&nbsp;	net cash generated by the company’s operations,&nbsp;<br><br>earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortisation&nbsp;<br>useful for determining how much debt a company can sustain.<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Finance costs&nbsp;	interest that the company has incurred on its borrowings – that may be in the form of bonds or may be bank loans and overdrafts.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Profit Before Tax	profit made by the company in the period, before considering any tax that may be payable&nbsp; (net)	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Taxation	corporation tax charge that the company has incurred for the period.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Net Income	revenue -&nbsp;tax and financing costs&nbsp;<br><br>Net income reflects all of the revenues earned during the period, less all of the expenditures incurred. This net income is also the profit attributable to the shareholders of the company because, in theory, it could all be distributed to shareholders as dividends.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Earnings Per Share (EPS)	amount of profit per share in market -&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	EPS equation	EPS&nbsp; =&nbsp; (Net income for the financial year – Dividends on preferred shares) / Number of ordinary shares in issue	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Dividends	profit paid to shareholders based on the amount they invested	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	order of payment - dvidends	preference shareholders are paid out first, followed by dividends to ordinary shareholders at an amount set by the board.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	how are dividends paid	dividends for most listed companies are either paid in two or four instalments: either a single interim dividend paid after the half-year stage or three quarterly dividends, and a final dividend to be paid after the accounts have been approved.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Capital expenditure	money spent to buy non-current assets, such as PPE. It is reflected on the statement of financial position.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Revenue expenditure	money spent that immediately impacts the income statement. Examples of revenue expenditure include wages paid to staff, rent paid on property and professional fees, like audit fees.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	cash flow statement	summary of all the payments and receipts that have occurred over the course of the year,&nbsp;<br><br>statement of cash flows is required by accounting standard IAS 7.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	reason for cash flow statement	enables readers&nbsp;to see clearly how cash has been generated and/or used over the course of the year.&nbsp;<br><br>provide relatively easily understood information to the users of the accounts&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	IAS 7 cash flow headers	IAS 7 requires headers to label cash flows, such as&nbsp;<br><br>Cash receipts from customers<br><br>Cash paid to suppliers and employees<br><br>Cash generated from operations<br><br>Tax paid<br><br>Interest paid<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Operating activities - header	cash that has been generated from the trading activities of the company. cash from sales, cash paid as salaries etc	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Investing activities	investment income&nbsp; as cash over the year - interest and dividends	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Financing activities<br>	cash spent during the year on paying dividends to shareholders,&nbsp;<br>borrowing on a long-term basis or the cash raised from issuing shares,&nbsp;<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Profit on cash statement	appears in the income statement and is the excess of revenues earned over the period, after expenses incurred	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	how is cash generated	<b>when cash received exceeds cash paid out,&nbsp;</b>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	how is cash used up	cash is used up when the cash paid out exceeds the cash received.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	importance of cash	described as the lifeblood of the company. Without it, the company will not survive. If a company does not have the cash to pay a liability when it is due, there is a possibility of the company being forced to close down.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	profit vs cash	profit is based on revenues earned, not cash received,&nbsp;<br><br>company may make sales on credit and, therefore, recognise the revenues at the point of sale in the income statement. The cash for those sales could be received significantly later.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	what is profit based on	profit is based on expenditure incurred, not cash paid,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	example of profit vs cash	treatments of the purchase of a non-current tangible asset, like a machine.&nbsp;<br><br>In the income statement, the impact will be a gradual expense incurred each year for the depreciation of the machine.&nbsp;<br><br>In the cash flow statement, the full cost will be paid in cash at the time of purchase.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	free cash flow.&nbsp;	represent the amount of cash that has been generated and that the company can choose what to do with. liquidity buffer	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	(enterprise cash flow),&nbsp;	free cash flow to the firm&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	(equity cash flow).&nbsp;	free cash flow to the shareholders&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	how is the free cash flow calculated	calculated from the income statement<br><br>generally arrived at by taking the net income from the income statement, adding back the charges for depreciation and amortisation and deducting capital expenditure.&nbsp;<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	enterprise cash flow&nbsp;	free cash flow before considering payments made to any of the providers of finance to the firm.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	PPE	plant, property and equipment	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	annual report and accounts.&nbsp;	addition to the financial statements, this document also includes other information that may be helpful to the many interested parties.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Strategic report	where the directors of the company (particularly the chairman and the CEO) report on the strategy that the company has adopted, including any major plans for the future.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Stakeholder review	<b>looks at the major stakeholders (including customers, employees, society in general, the environment and shareholders) and the way the company is looking after their interests.&nbsp;</b>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Financial review	(CFO) will outline the company’s performance based on a variety of metrics,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Governance report	outline how the company runs the business responsibly and effectively including individual reports from sub-committees of the board such as the audit committee.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Directors’ remuneration	directors are rewarded is something investors should want to know.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Sustainability report	increasingly public companies are including a section in their annual reports to discuss how the company attempts to minimise environmental impacts,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Independent auditor’s report&nbsp;	report done externally by auditors (EY/KPMG etc)&nbsp;<br><br>gives an opinion on whether the financial statements have been properly prepared and present a true&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	purpose of ratio analysis	assessing business performance,&nbsp;identifying meaningful relationships between numbers contained within company financial statements,&nbsp;<br><br>Summarise financial information&nbsp;<br><br>Identify trends, strengths and weaknesses by comparing the ratios&nbsp;<br><br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	limitations of ratio analysis	financial statements contain historic data, ratios are not predictive;&nbsp;<br><br>alternative accounting methods and differences in international accounting practices may make it difficult to draw comparisons.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Profitability ratios&nbsp;	representation of the percentage return that the company generates relative to its revenues.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	gross profit&nbsp;	percentage of revenues that the company earns after considering the costs of sales.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	operating profit margin&nbsp;	percentage of revenues that the company earns after considering costs of sales and other operating costs&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	net profit margin&nbsp;	percentage of revenues that the company earns after considering all costs&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Return on capital employed (ROCE)&nbsp;	looks at the amount of return (profit) that is being generated as a percentage of the finance put into the business&nbsp;<br><br>amount of capital employed is the equity plus the long-term debt.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	return on assets (ROA)&nbsp;	commentators refer to ROCE as the return on assets (ROA)&nbsp;<br><br>capital employed in the business is, by definition, equivalent to the net assets being used by the business.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	return on shareholders’ equity (ROE)&nbsp;	net income generated as a percentage of the equity financing the business.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	ROCE equation	(Operating profit / Capital employed) x 100&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	ROA equation (return on assets)	(Operating profit / Total net assets) x 100	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	ROE (return on shareholders equity) equation	(Net income / Shareholders' equity) x 100	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Gross profit margin (%) equation	(Gross profit / Revenues) x 100&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Operating profit margin (%) equation	(Operating profit / Revenues) x 100	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Asset turnover&nbsp;	annual revenues (the turnover of the business) and dividing it by the total net assets in the business.&nbsp;<br><br>expressed as a number of times (number of times revenue generated exceeded company assets)	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Asset turnover (number of times) equation	Revenues / Total net assets	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	asset turnover significance	The larger the asset turnover, the more use the company is getting from the assets.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Financial gearing&nbsp;	measure of risk arising from the company’s debt.&nbsp;<br><br>also called ‘financial leverage’,&nbsp;<br><br>leverage is determined by examining the amount of a company’s financing that comes from debt, and the amount that comes from shareholders’ funds or equity – the debt to equity ratio.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	gearing ratio significance	higher the proportion of debt finance, the higher the risk that the company will not be able to meet its financial commitments.&nbsp;<br><br>interest on debt must be paid every year and the debt must be repaid at some point, whereas dividends on shares need only be paid in profitable years and share capital never has to be repaid.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Debt to Equity&nbsp;&nbsp;	ratio of how much debt compared to how much equity a company carries<br><br>All interest-bearing non-current liabilities are generally considered to be debt,&nbsp;<br><br>= Debt/Equity	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Net Debt	debt as in the debt-to-equity ratio, less the cash and short-term investments that are within the current assets on the statement of financial position.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Net Debt to Equity	(Debt less cash and short-term investments)/Equity	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Interest Cover&nbsp;	Operating Profit/Interest Costs	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Liquidity Ratios	ratio of liabilities to liquidity (cash) that can be used to pay it	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Current Ratio	current ratio uses numbers from the statement of financial position <br><br>&nbsp;divides a company’s current assets by its current liabilities&nbsp;<br><br>Current ratio = Current assets / Current liabilities	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	current ratio significance	higher the result, the more readily a company should be able to meet its liabilities&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Quick Ratio (Acid Test)	excludes inventory from the current assets figure, as inventory is potentially noy liquid (cant sell quick)<br><br>Quick ratio = (Current assets – Inventory) / Current liabilities<br><br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	what do investors ratios establish	how expensive the shares are, in order to reach a conclusion on the likelihood of capital growth,&nbsp;<br><br>how much in dividends the shares pay, and how easily the company is able to bear the payment&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Earnings Per Share (EPS)	EPS = Net profit or loss attributable to ordinary shareholders / Average weighted number of ordinary shares outstanding in a period<br><br><br>net profit or loss attributable to ordinary shareholders will be the net profit shown on the income statement, less than any dividends on preference shares for the year.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	why is EPS useful	EPS reveals how much profit was made during the year that is available to be paid out to each share.&nbsp;<br><br>it can be divided into the current share price to assess how many times the profit per share must be paid to buy a share&nbsp;<div>This is the price earnings ratio (P/E).</div><div><br></div>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	EPS with preference shares	EPS = (Net income for the financial year – Dividends on preferred shares) / Number of ordinary shares in issue	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Diluted Earnings Per Share purpose	warn shareholders of potential future changes in the EPS figure as a result of events&nbsp;<br><br>DEPS is a diluted EPS figure considering potential future risks	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Diluted Earnings Per Share use	useful if company has a lot of share options/convertible bonds issued, could dilute share price<br><br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	p/e ratio calculation	P/E Ratio = Current market price per share / Earnings per share<br><br>suppose that the ordinary shares for a company are currently trading at €2.50 per share and that the EPS is 20c: the P/E ratio is €2.50 / €0.20 = 12.5.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	P/E ratio	number of years of earnings at the current level to generate the share price,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Trailing&nbsp; (P/E)	backward-looking version of P/E that has already been described in the formula.&nbsp;<br><br>Also known as P/E trailing twelve months (ttm), or last twelve months (ltm),&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Forward&nbsp; P/E	as estimated P/E, and is based on estimation of net earnings over the next 12 months.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Companies with losses P/E	undefined P/E ratio (usually shown as not applicable (N/A) or not meaningful (nm)).	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Uses of the P/E Ratio	analyse the market’s stock valuation of a company and its shares, relative to the income the company is actually generating.&nbsp;<br><br>higher (and/or more certain) forecast earnings growth will usually have a higher P/E,&nbsp;<br><br>those expected to have lower (and/or riskier) earnings growth will, in most cases, have a lower P/E.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	use pf P/E ratio	compare the relative valuations of stocks.&nbsp;<br><br>If one stock has a P/E twice that of another stock, all things being equal, it is a less attractive investment.&nbsp;<br><br>companies are rarely equal and care needs to be taken when making comparisons. P/E ratios between countries, industries, companies of different size, and during different time periods can vary significantly.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Sectors P/E significance	market’s expectations as to future earnings growth in different sectors.&nbsp;<br><br>relatively young technology company which has bright prospects will often be rewarded by investors with a relatively high P/E ratio as the earnings are expected to grow dynamically.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	interest rates relation to stock valuation	During periods when short-term interest rates are relatively low, and inflation is considered to be benign, a larger P/E ratio is supportable&nbsp;<br><br>this is as equities are favoured to fixed income for returns<br><br>interest rates will lower the discount factor for future cash flows, thereby increasing the net present value of an investment in the firm.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	EV&nbsp; enterprise value	measure of a company’s value and is often used as an alternative to straightforward market capitalisation.&nbsp;<br><br>firm’s EV is calculated as its <br><br>market capitalisation + outstanding debt + non-controlling interests + preferred shares - all of the cash and cash equivalents.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	EV&nbsp; definition	sum of the claims of all of a company’s security holders, which includes all of the debt holders, preferred shareholders, minority shareholders, common equity holders, and others.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	EV in simplified terms	consider it as the cost of purchasing an entire business. If you settle with all the security holders, you have essentially purchased the company at its EV.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	EBIT&nbsp;	same as profit before interest and tax (PBIT) (operating profit)<br><br>EBIT = Revenue less expenses (excluding tax and interest)	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	EBITDA&nbsp;	income before interest and tax, but with depreciation, and amortisation reversed and added back.&nbsp;<br><br>simplistic operating cash flow from the business, as depreciation and amortisation do not involve the movement of cash.&nbsp;<br><br>EBITDA = Revenue less expenses (excluding tax, interest, depreciation and amortisation)	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	issue with EBITDA	EBITDA does not truly represent cash earnings: it leaves out the cash required to fund working capital and the replacement of old equipment, which can be significant.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Enterprise Value (EV) to Earnings Before Interest, Tax, Depreciation and Amortisation (EBITDA)	used as a measure of the relative expensiveness (or cheapness) of a business and also uses the EV as the numerator in the ratio.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	gross dividend yield&nbsp;	total dividends per share paid over the last year as a percentage of the current share price.<br><br>Gross dividend yield = (Dividend / Current share price) x 100	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	example of gross dividend yield	let us assume that the board of directors of a company with five million shares in issue decides to distribute €400,000 to shareholders in the form of a dividend and retain the rest of the net income on its statement of financial position. <br><br>The dividend per share will be €400,000 divided by five million shares = 8c. Assume the shares are currently trading at $1.60.<br><br>Gross dividend yield = (8 / 160) x 100 = 5%	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	use of gross dividend yield	high yield may indicate that the share price is relatively low in comparison with the return it offers.&nbsp;<br><br>suggests that the market does not have confidence that the dividends paid in the past will continue to be paid in the future.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	low dividend yield meaning	indicates high market confidence in the company’s ability to increase dividends.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	Gross Dividend Cover	used to assess how well a company covered its dividend payout with the profits it made.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	dividend cover&nbsp;	how easy was it for the company to pay these dividends?	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	how is dividend cover calculated	compares the earnings of the company&nbsp;with the dividends paid in the year. This also reveals the proportion of profits that were reinvested in the company.&nbsp;<br><br>If dividend cover was two times, then half of the profits are paid out to shareholders and half are retained.<br><br>Dividend cover = EPS / Dividends per share	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	dividend cover example	Assuming an EPS of 18c and a dividend of 8c per share, the dividend cover will be 18 / 8 = 2.25 times.<br><br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES CHAPTER 8: ACCOUNTING ANALYSIS	uncovered dividend,&nbsp;	A dividend cover of less than one is known as an uncovered dividend, meaning the year’s profits were not enough to cover the dividend.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	CORNERSTONES OF INVESTMENT THEORY	what is the expected return? Often based upon a probability study, and just as important, what is the notion of the risk associated with achieving that return?&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	systematic/market risk	risk that the overall market in general, or the relevant part of the capital markets for investors wanting exposure to specific sectors, will rise or fall,&nbsp;<br><br>Because all securities are part of some broad asset class and this grouping is often the primary driver of returns, this type of risk is difficult to avoid, even with diversification.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	specific risk	unsystematic risk<br><br>risk that something adverse impacts the value of a particular investment,&nbsp;<br><br><br>company’s management making some sort of error&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	inflation Risk	Inflation will erode returns or purchasing power and even if the investor has taken account of inflation in their analysis the actual and expected inflation may be different from that assumed in calculating ERs.&nbsp;<br><br>as FI products pay a fixed return - more prevalent there	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Interest Rate Risk	Changes in interest rates will affect prices;&nbsp;<br><br>investments with longer terms to maturity and lower coupon rates are more effected by interest rate risk<br><br>higher inflation tends to push interest rates higher.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	interest rate effect on equities	present the risk that firms carrying large amounts of debt (particularly short-term or floating rate) will need to refinance at much higher rates, or pay much more to service the debt.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Reinvestment Risk	risk that an investor cannot invest maturing assets at the same rate as originally obtained. - linked to interest rate risks.<br><br>rise in interest rate can cause FI to fall - unable to reinvest at same level	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Exchange Rate Risk	investor who purchases securities which are denominated in a foreign currency may suffer (or benefit) from changes in the exchange rates between the home or base currency or the other currency.&nbsp;<br><br>carry trade in yen<br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Political and Legal Risk	changes to the political or legal landscape&nbsp;<br><br>changes could affect the economy where investments are made or could be particular to overseas investors.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Regulatory Risk	risk that a change in regulations or legislation will affect a security, company, or industry.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Default Risk	investor may find that a company from which they have purchased a security could become insolvent due to a harsh operating environment, high levels of borrowing, poor management and other financial miscalculations.&nbsp;<br><br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Liquidity Risk	risk there's no liquidity to sell investment at a price you will make profit	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Forward-looking forecasts	probabilities assess the likelihood of each possible state of the world occurring and estimate the returns and values arising given that particular outcome.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Backward-looking analyses&nbsp;	study historically observed returns and associated frequencies on the assumption that this past data will be representative of the future.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Equities&nbsp;	shares in companies that give the investor an ownership stake in the company, alongside the attraction of limited liability.&nbsp;<br><br>shareholders’ loss is simply the amount paid for the shares.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Risk Profile for Equities	riskier than FI due to no guaranteed payment, however higher levels of potential returns	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Equity Risk Premium	a higher rate of return that is required to entice investors to take on the risk of owning shares or equity, as opposed to holding a more secure asset such as a bond. the amount paid above the risk free rate&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	risk-free rate&nbsp;	rate on government bonds and short-term debts, because of the low chance that the government will default on its loans.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	example of&nbsp;Equity Risk Premium	total return on a stock is 10% over a given period and the risk-free rate over the same period is 4%, the equity-risk premium is 6%.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Money Market Instruments	low-risk, relatively secure and deliver income, but provide little scope for capital growth.&nbsp;<br><br>investment horizons that are very short (eg, the next six months rather than the next 20 years),&nbsp;<br><br>potential for investors to keep their funds in cash and place them on interest-earning deposit,&nbsp;<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	prices of money market instruments	can&nbsp;fluctuate can become quite erratic, in moments of financial crisis. Many investors will want to hold the shortest-term instruments from the most secure issuers, such as T-bills, during periods when the money markets are not functioning normally.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Bonds&nbsp;	described as ‘fixed interest’ or ‘fixed income’, meaning there is a contractually fixed rate of return to the investor.&nbsp;<br><br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	rate of return on bonds	contractually fixed rate is also a fixed percentage (such as 5%), but in other (less common) instances, it is fixed by reference to a published interest rate (such as a market reference rate plus 0.5%).&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	‘floating’ rate&nbsp;	when fixed to published reference rate plus bps,&nbsp;because the reference rate can, and does, vary over time.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	who issues bonds	issued by companies, governments, government agencies, state-owned enterprises, and supranationals, such as the World Bank, the Asian Infrastructure Investment Bank (AIIB) and the African Development Bank (AfDB).	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	why are bonds attractive investments	offer from regular, pre- determined coupons, combined with the relative certainty of the principal amount to be repaid.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	index linked bonds	coupons tied to inflation - real rate stays the same<br><br><br>The principal amount paid at maturity is generally the par or nominal value, although with index-linked bonds it will be uplifted for inflation.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	bonds vs equities - inflation	Bonds are generally less risky than equities, but offer less potential for substantial returns.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	interest rate risk&nbsp;	risk that an interest rate movement brings about an adverse movement in the value of an investment.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	interest rate risk effected on&nbsp;fixed-interest bond&nbsp;	Because of the inverse relationship between bonds and interest rates, the value of the bond will fall. Interest rate risk is largely removed if the bond is floating-rate, since the coupon will be reset in line with the higher market interest rate.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Inflation risk&nbsp;	arises when inflation is more substantial than the investor expected, and the value of the investments held may fall.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Inflation risk effect on bonds	bonds will suffer because the fixed cash payments that they deliver are less valuable. Floating-rate bonds will suffer less because the higher inflation will bring about a higher interest rate,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	default risk	bond issuer fails - more corporate bonds as gov bonds are backed by government - countered by ratings	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	investment grade rating	one that is at least BBB (from S&amp;P or Fitch Ratings), or at least Baa from Moody’s. Issues of bonds categorised as sub-investment grade are alternatively known as junk bonds because of the high levels of credit risk.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	what happens when rating agency downgrades	potential investors will look to compensate for the increased risk by demanding a greater yield on the issuer’s bonds. This will inevitably result in a lower price for the bond.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	conservative investor&nbsp;	will hold a greater proportion of low-risk bonds and money market instruments. These lower-risk investments are likely to give rise to lower, but more predictable, returns.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	adventurous investor&nbsp;	hold a greater proportion of medium- and high-risk equity investments, because higher risk means greater potential for higher returns.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	diversification&nbsp;	combining securities that are not perfectly positively correlated into a portfolio, which will remove some of the unsystematic risk in that portfolio.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Correlation	assets which move alongside eachother&nbsp;<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	correlation example	assets A and B have a tendency to react to the same kinds of business conditions in a very simple and predictable manner they could be said to be strongly correlated.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	uncorrelated returns&nbsp;	returns do not tend to move in the same direction and to the same degree	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Diversification benefits&nbsp;	maximised by holding investments with uncorrelated returns&nbsp;<br><br>can remove some or most of the security-specific risk and, therefore, the overall risk of the portfolio, while retaining the same level of return.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Diversification by Asset Class	for example – cash, fixed-income securities, equity investments, collective investment schemes, derivatives, real estate or property-based investments, and other assets.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	cash usefulness	useful as an emergency fund or for instantly accessible money.&nbsp;<br><br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Fixed-income securities, usefuleness	such as government bonds and high-grade corporate, give a secure income and known redemption value at a fixed future date.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	equities usefulness in portfolio	produce dividend income and capital growth. For example, a share yielding 3% income (dividend yield) plus capital growth of 6% (share price rise) gives an overall return of 9% compared with a bank deposit account yielding, say, 4%.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	mutual funds, or ETFs&nbsp;	represent pools of investments, they offer a certain degree of diversification on their own.&nbsp;<br><br>exposure to some of the lesser understood world markets where there is a high risk in holding just one company’s shares.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Derivatives&nbsp;	options, futures or forwards are often used by institutional investors to provide exposure to various investment types,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Diversification by Maturity	used in FI,&nbsp;known as ‘laddering’.&nbsp;<br><br>spreading maturities across a period of time will potentially reduce risk (reinvestment risk) for a fixed-income portfolio.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Hedging&nbsp;	attempt to reduce risk, usually achieved by using derivatives, for example, options, futures and forwards.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	objective of hedging	to buy or sell derivatives that reduce the exposure to market fluctuations that would take place in the portfolio. This is done by taking the opposite position of what is in the portfolio with the derivative.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	example of hedging with derivatives	buying put options (the right to sell) on investments held in the portfolio will enable the investor to remove the risk of a fall in value. However, the investor will have to pay a premium to buy the options.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	how can futures used to hedge	can be used to hedge against equity prices falling – but the futures contracts will remove any upside as well as downside.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	how can forwards reduce currency risk	lock in exchange rate now for transaction later<br><br>Currency forwards could be used to eliminate exchange rate risk – but, like futures, the upside potential will be lost in order to hedge against the downside risk.&nbsp;<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	most commonly used techniques for hedging a portfolio&nbsp;	through selling futures contracts to control the level of exposure arising from the ownership of equities.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	advantages of futures	advantages of lower cost, greater efficiency and less portfolio disruption.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	problems with futures	may not be able to hedge all risk if no specific contract for that<br><br>portfolio which has a combination of small-capitalisation stocks and emerging market exchange-traded funds will be difficult to hedge by selling a broad-based futures contract on, say, the FTSE 100 index or the S&amp;P 500.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Hedging with Options	continue to hold the investments,&nbsp;and to purchase put options on those positions (or on an index where the performance of an index closely matches the overall portfolio).&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	put option	A put option, when purchased, gives the owner the right, but not the obligation to sell the underlying equities at a predetermined price.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	why are put options used	combination of owning, or being long on both the investment(s) and the puts enables the holder to continue to participate in any upside potential of the investments, while enjoying the right to sell the investment at the predetermined price&nbsp;<br><br>provides the protection from excessive loss,&nbsp;<br><br>The fund manager will cap his or her loss at the strike price less the cost to purchase the put option contracts.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	motivation behind buying a put option&nbsp;	protect against a fall in the price of the underlying asset.&nbsp;<br><br>The holder of a put obtains the right, but not the obligation, to sell at a fixed price. The value of this right will become increasingly valuable as the asset price falls.&nbsp;<br><br>The greatest profit that will arise from buying a put will be achieved if the asset price falls to zero.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	wasting asset&nbsp;	the premium paid for the put option&nbsp;<br><br>it is subject to decay as the expiration of the option period approaches.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Contracts for differences (CFDs)&nbsp;	originally developed in the early 1990s and were initially used by institutional investors to hedge their exposure to stocks in a cost-effective way.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	how is price of CFD formed	price of the CFD tracks the price of the underlying asset,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	how does holder of CFD benefit	benefits, or loses, from the price movement in the stock, bond, currency, commodity or index. But the CFD holder does not take ownership of the underlying asset.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	margin-traded,&nbsp;	the investor does not have to deposit the full value of the underlying asset with the CFD provider.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	why do investors buy CFDs	buy exposure to market movements, using only a fraction of the capital they would require in the cash market. The investor then has a geared position relative to the capital deposited.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	how are CFDs used for hedging	enable the fund manager to retain a position in the cash instrument but have a derivative position which is equivalent to that of short-selling the stock.&nbsp;<br><br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	costs of CFDs&nbsp;	comprise commissions for each deal, plus a cost built into the spread of the CFD price, together with a funding/financing charge.&nbsp;<br><br>The parties to a CFD pay to finance long positions and may receive funding on short positions in lieu of deferring sale proceeds. The contracts are settled for the cash differential between the price of the opening and closing trades.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Investors in CFDs requirements	maintain a certain amount of margin as defined by the brokerage – usually ranging from 1% to 30%.&nbsp;<br><br>a leveraged position in a volatile CFD can expose the buyer to further margin calls in a downturn, which will fund what may crystallise as a substantial loss.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	why do CFDs allow	allow a trader to go short or long on any position using margin.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Initial margin	normally between 5% and 30% for shares/stocks and 1% for indices and foreign exchange.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Variation or maintenance margin	CFD will be marked to the market at currently prevailing prices and if the position has moved beyond the amount taken as initial margin – ie, the position has moved adversely – additional margin would be required. This is termed variation or maintenance margin.

CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	examples of institutional investors	pension funds, the providers of life assurance, the providers of general insurance and banks.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	pension funds aim	set up with the aim of providing retirement funds for the beneficiaries. They may be sponsored by an employer, be solely dependent on contributions from the workforce, or a combination of the two.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	how pension funds are invested	accumulate income and capital gains tax-free. The money in the fund is invested by fund managers and, because pension funds have a relatively long investment horizon, they can take risks and have traditionally tended to invest heavily in equities.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	(defined contribution schemes).&nbsp;	benefit is driven by the contributions made and the investment performance&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	(defined benefit schemes&nbsp;	those that define the benefits they will pay out&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Life assurance business&nbsp;	insurance contracts written by an insurance company on the life of an individual.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	term assurance policies	in exchange for a regular premium, only pay out if the individual dies&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	whole of life policies	pay out on death in exchange for regular premiums	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	endowment policies	term assurance policies with a significant investment element that depends on the performance of the insurance company’s fund	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	single premium life assurance bonds	single premium endowments; again they have a significant investment element.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	General insurance&nbsp;	insurance is written by an insurance company against short-term personal and commercial risks,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Hedge funds&nbsp;	unauthorised investment vehicles that are not subject to the same regulatory oversight as other pooled funds.&nbsp;<br><br>invest in a wide variety of investments in different markets, and to employ high-risk strategies, including gearing with derivatives and seeking arbitrage opportunities.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	objective of hedge funds&nbsp;	produce positive returns, no matter what the general market is doing.&nbsp;<br><br>hedge funds often promote themselves as ‘absolute return’ vehicles, meaning they expect to earn positive returns in all market conditions.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	ESG FUNDS	sustainable investments enabling investors to easily gain access to investments complying with appropriate environmental, social and governance (ESG) standards to create a more responsible portfolio.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Sovereign wealth funds (SWFs)&nbsp;	state-owned investment funds commonly established from balance of payments surpluses, the proceeds of privatisations, fiscal surpluses and/or receipts from resource exports.<br><br>prefer returns over liquidity, therefore they often have a relatively high risk tolerance compared to many traditional funds.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	sovereign wealth fund objectives&nbsp;	Protecting &amp; stabilising the budget&nbsp;<br><br>Diversifying from non-renewable commodity exports.<br><br>Earning greater returns than on foreign exchange&nbsp;<br><br>Increasing savings for future generations.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	world’s largest SWFs&nbsp;	Norges Bank Investment Management (NBIM)<br><br>State Administration of Foreign Exchange (SAFE)<br><br>China Investment Corporation (CIC)&nbsp;<br><br>Abu Dhabi Investment Authority (ADIA)<br><br>Saudi Arabian Public Investment Fund (PIF)<br><br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Norges Bank Investment Management (NBIM)	set up to shield the Norwegian economy from ups and downs in oil revenue.&nbsp;<br><br>AuM: Approx. $1.945 trillion.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	State Administration of Foreign Exchange (SAFE)	invests in international bonds, equities and alternative assets through subsidiaries&nbsp;<br><br>AuM: Approx. $1.582 trillion.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	China Investment Corporation&nbsp;	China’s flagship SWF with a mission to diversity China’s foreign exchange holdings&nbsp;<br><br>AuM: Approx. $1.332 trillion.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Abu Dhabi Investment Authority (ADIA)	funded from the budgetary surpluses originated from oil exports. Due to its size and significant allocation to private markets, ADIA is one of the world’s largest investors in real estate, infrastructure and private equity. AuM: Approx. $993 billion.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Saudi Arabian Public Investment Fund (PIF)	AuM: Approx. $925 billion.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	active management	manager’s strategy is designed to outperform the returns available from an investment benchmark index.&nbsp;<br><br>constant activity vs sit and wait	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	example of active management	an active portfolio manager focused on investing in large-cap UK equities will be seeking to realise returns superior to those available from a simple method/strategy of buying and holding all of the constituents of the FTSE 100.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	how could active managers pick investments	could focus on selecting securities based on research and quantitative analysis&nbsp;<br>such as P/E ratios and price earnings growth (PEG) ratios,&nbsp;<br><br>sector investments that attempt to anticipate long-term macro-economic trends&nbsp;<br>focus on energy or technology stocks),&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	advantages of active management	allows selection of a variety of investments instead of investing in the market as a whole.&nbsp;<br>may also want to reduce volatility by investing in less risky, high-quality companies, rather than in the market as a whole, even at the cost of slightly lower returns.&nbsp;<br><br><br>Some investors may also wish to follow a strategy that avoids or underweights certain industries compared to the market as a whole, for instance, an employee of a high-technology growth company who receives company stock or stock options as a benefit might prefer not to have additional funds invested in the same industry.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	disadvantages in active management	fund manager may make bad investment choices or follow an unsound theory in managing the portfolio. The fees associated with active management are also higher than those associated with passive management<br><br>strategies that involve frequent trading generate higher transaction costs which diminish the fund’s return.&nbsp;<br><br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	pseudo-tracker,&nbsp;	When the assortment of assets of an actively managed fund becomes too large, it inevitably begins to take on index-like characteristics as it must invest in an increasingly broad selection of securities which will tend to perform exactly in line with the overall market.<br><br>paying active management fees when the actual style is effectively passive.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Active Management with Manager Participation	when the managers/directors have a vested interest in the success of the fund.&nbsp;<br><br>private equity funds and hedge funds often provide real active management.&nbsp;<br><br>manager’s interests will be aligned with those of the investors. The manager has what is known as ‘skin in the game’.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	active bond strategies	used by those portfolio managers who believe the bond market is not perfectly efficient and, therefore, is subject to mispricing. If a bond is considered mispriced, then active management strategies can be employed to capitalise upon this perceived pricing anomaly.<br><br>Bond switching, or bond swapping, is used by those portfolio managers who believe they can outperform a buy-and-hold passive policy,&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	bond switching -&nbsp;Anomaly switching	involves moving between two bonds similar in all respects apart from the yield and price on which each trades.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Policy switching -&nbsp;Bond switching&nbsp;	when an interest rate cut is expected but not implied by the yield curve, shorter- dated, low-duration bonds are sold in favour of longer-dated, high-duration bonds.&nbsp;<br>pre-empting the rate cut, the holder can subsequently benefit from the greater price volatility of the latter bonds.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Inter-market spread switch -&nbsp;Bond switching&nbsp;	that the difference in the yield being offered between corporate bonds and comparable government bonds,&nbsp;<br>government bonds will be purchased in favour of corporate bonds.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Riding the yield curve&nbsp;	takes advantage of an upward-sloping yield curve.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	‘market timing’.	if it is believed the market’s view on future interest rate movements, implied by the yield curve, are incorrect or have failed to be anticipated.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Passive managers&nbsp;	follow exactly the course just outlined and invest in an index fund or derivative that replicates as closely as possible the investment weighting and returns of a selected benchmark index, such as the FTSE 100 in the UK&nbsp;<br><br>also available for many emerging markets, including South Africa, Egypt and Nigeria in Africa; India, China, Indonesia, Philippines, Sri Lanka and Vietnam in Asia; and Mexico, Brazil, Peru and Chile in Latin America.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	advantage of a passive approach	performance of the fund is not dependent on the manager’s ability to make investment choices&nbsp;<br><br>investor is only exposed to the systematic risk within the market and can avoid the risks (and potential rewards) of non-systematic risk.<br><br>avoid incurring the fees associated with active management.&nbsp;<br><br>total expense ratio will be considerably lower than for many actively managed funds, such as hedge funds which operate with fees charged for funds under management and also incentive fees.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Disadvantages of passive management&nbsp;	performance is always dictated by a benchmark or index, meaning that investors must be satisfied with the index returns.&nbsp;<br><br>prevents defensive measures if it appears that a certain class of asset prices, specifically equities, may be heading for turbulent trading&nbsp;<br><br>lacks the potential to generate alpha	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	alpha	return on investment excessive to a risk adjusted benchmark return	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	beta	returns on a stock: returns of the market<br><br>measure of systematic risk of portfolio compared to a market	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	beta	measure of the sensitivity of an investment’s movements in relation to the overall market.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	beta example	The market has a beta of precisely one, and an index that tracks the market exactly would also have a beta of one. A security with a beta greater than one would be more volatile (risky) than the overall market, and a beta with less than one would be less volatile (risky).&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Smart beta&nbsp;	investment strategy that does not use the traditional market capitalisation weighting system, but instead uses alternative weighting systems,&nbsp;<br><br>for example weighting constituents equally, or based on dividends paid, sales revenues or cash flow generation.&nbsp;<br><br>taking the key advantage of active investment management by introducing the possibility of outperformance, and combining it with the key advantages of passive index tracking by creating a well- diversified portfolio at a lower cost than active management.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Passive bond&nbsp;	employed either when the market is believed to be efficient, in which case a buy-and-hold strategy is used, or when a bond portfolio is constructed around meeting a future liability fixed in nominal terms.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Immunisation&nbsp;	passive management technique employed by those bond portfolio managers with a known future liability to meet. An immunised bond portfolio is one that is insulated from the effect of future interest rate changes.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Cash matching -&nbsp;Immunisation&nbsp;	constructing a bond portfolio whose coupon and redemption payment cash flows are synchronised to match those of the liabilities to be met.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Duration-based immunisation	constructing a bond portfolio with the same initial value as the present value of the liability it is designed to meet and the same duration as this liability.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	barbell strategy&nbsp;	hold bonds with different maturity's e.g. 5,10,15 (weighted on each side)<br><br>Barbell portfolios necessarily require more frequent rebalancing than bullet portfolios.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	ladder portfolio&nbsp;	equal amounts invested in bonds with different durations. So, for a liability with a ten-year duration, an appropriate ladder strategy may be to hold equal amounts in bonds with a one-year duration, two-year duration and so on right through to 20 years.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	efficient markets hypothesis (EMH)&nbsp;	investment theory stating that it is impossible to beat the market because stock market efficiency causes existing share prices to always incorporate and reflect all relevant information.&nbsp;<br><br>this means that stocks always trade at their fair value on stock exchanges, making it impossible for investors to either purchase undervalued stocks or sell stocks for inflated prices.&nbsp;As such, it should be impossible to outperform the overall market through expert stock selection, technical analysis or market timing, and the only way an investor can possibly obtain higher returns is by purchasing riskier investments.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	active manager strategies to be EMH	outperforming a designated benchmark by seeking out market inefficiencies and by purchasing securities that are undervalued, or by short-selling securities that are overvalued.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Weak form EMH&nbsp;	recognises that differences in the spread of information (particularly through proprietary research) can offer advantages, and the fact that trading or structural costs may make some strategies profitable for some traders but not for others.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	ESG investing&nbsp;	&nbsp;Environmental, social and governance (ESG) the level to which these factors are considered in selecting and managing investments.&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	ESG investing is also known as&nbsp;	as responsible investing (RI), social responsible investing (SRI), impact investing or sustainable investing	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	growth in ESG investing&nbsp;	accelerated both by prominent international organisations like the United Nations (UN) and the Financial Stability Board (FSB)&nbsp;	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	principles for responsible investment (PRI).&nbsp;	independent not-for-profit organisation (NPO) that engages with global policymakers and encourages investors to use responsible investment to enhance returns and better manage risks.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	what are the 6 PRI	we will incorporate ESG issues into investment analysis and decision-making processes.<br><br>we will be active owners and incorporate ESG issues into our ownership policies and practices.<br><br>we will seek appropriate disclosure on ESG issues by the entities in which we invest.<br><br>we will promote acceptance and implementation of the Principles within the investment industry.&nbsp;<br><br>we will work together to enhance our effectiveness in implementing the Principles.<br><br>we will each report on our activities and progress towards implementing the Principles.<br><br>	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	perceived advantages of adopting an ESG approach&nbsp;	sustainable and ESG funds have shown a lower downside risk compared to traditional funds,&nbsp;<br><br>Companies and funds adopting an ESG approach will be better placed to attract and retain investors.	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	disadvantages to following an ESG-driven approach&nbsp;	Investment choices that align with their values might replace opportunities that are likely to make them a profit.<br><br>requires more research<br><br>Greenwashing	
CISI BOOK 1: SECURITIES CHAPTER 9: INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT	Greenwashing	funds that claim to be socially or environmentally responsible may not be.&nbsp;

CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES: STRUGGLES	model 1 DVP	settles cash and securities on a gross trade by trade basis, with the final exchange occurring simultaneously - used in most countries/CREST

CISI BOOK 1: SECURTIES: STRUGGLES	how to calculate conversion premium	To calculate the premium, take the value of the bond (£107) and divide it by the value of the shares the bond could be converted into, ie, (50 x 185p) = £92.50. The answer is therefore 107 divided by 92.50 which is 1.157, so the bond is worth 1.157 times the value of the shares into which it could be converted: a 15.7% premium.
